H^{[1]}(\land Z)^{\vee}
by the dual of the holonomy representation, and

\bullet

L/I
acts in
I/I^{(2)}
by the representation induced from the right adjoint representation of
L
in
I
.

proof.
By definition,
H^{[1]}(\land Z,d_{Z})=Z\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{Z}
, while from Lemma
4.1
we obtain

s^{-1}(Z\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{Z})^{\vee}=L_{Z}/L_{Z}^{(2)}\hskip 5.69054pt=I/I^{(2)}.

It remains to verify that this isomorphism is an isomorphism of
L_{Z}/L_{Z}^{(2)}
-modules.

Denote the holonomy representation of
L/I
in
Z\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{Z}
by
\theta
. Then if
z\in Z\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{Z}
,

\overline{d}z=\sum\,w_{i}\otimes z_{i}

with
w_{i}\in W
and
z_{i}\in Z\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{Z}
. Thus (§5.3), for
y\in L
,
\theta(y)
is determined by the equation

<\theta(y)z,sx>=-\sum_{i}<w_{i},sy><z_{i},sx>=-<dz,sx,sy>.

Now it follows from Proposition
5.1
(iv) that

<\theta(y)z,sx>=<z,(-1)^{deg\,y}s[x,y]>=<z,\mbox{r ad}x(y)>.

\square

Part II
Profree Lie algebras

Free graded Lie algebras freely generated by a space
T
, and which we denote by
\mathbb{L}_{T}
, play a key role in Lie algebra theory. Profree Lie algebras are the analogue of free Lie algebras in the category of complete enriched Lie algebras. More precisely:

Definition
A
profree Lie algebra
is a complete enriched Lie algebra of the form
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
in which
(T,\{\rho_{\alpha}\})
is a complete enriched vector space and the enriched structure in
\mathbb{L}_{T}
is defined by the surjections

\mathbb{L}_{T}\to\mathbb{L}_{\rho_{\alpha}T}\to\mathbb{L}_{\rho_{\alpha}T}/\mathbb{L}^{n}_{\rho_{\alpha}T}.

We call
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
the profree Lie algebra generated by
T
.

Remark
; As observed in §1.1 the map
\mathbb{L}_{T}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is injective.

6
Characterization of profree Lie algebras

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
(T,\{\rho_{\alpha}\})
be a complete enriched vector space.

(i)

Any coherent linear map
f:T\to E
of degree
0
into a complete enriched Lie algebra
E
extends uniquely to a morphism

\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\to E

of complete enriched Lie algebras.

(ii)

\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}=\overline{\mathbb{L}}^{(2)}_{T}\oplus T.

(iii)

If
T^{\prime}
is any closed direct summand of
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
in
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
then the inclusion extends to an isomorphism

\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}

of complete enriched Lie algebras.

(iv)

If
T
is a graded vector space of finite type then the completion of the universal enveloping algebra
U\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is given by

\overline{U\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}}=\prod_{n\geq 0}\bigotimes^{n}(T)

where
\bigotimes^{n}
denotes the
n^{th}
tensor power.

proof.
(i) Denote by
E\to E_{\beta}
the surjections in the enriched structure of
E
. Since
E_{\beta}
is finite dimensional and nilpotent it follows that the extension of
f
to a morphism
\mathbb{L}_{T}\to E
factors to yield morphisms

\mathbb{L}_{\rho_{\alpha(\beta)}T}\to E_{\beta}.

Passing to inverse limits gives the morphism
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\to E
, and it is immediate that it is the unique extension of
f
.

(ii) Since
\mathbb{L}_{\rho_{\alpha}T}=\rho_{\alpha}T\oplus\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\rho_{\alpha}T}
it follows from Lemma
2.2
that

\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}=\varprojlim_{\alpha}\mathbb{L}_{\rho_{\alpha}}T/\rho_{\alpha}T=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/T.

This gives (ii).

(iii) If
T^{\prime}
is a second closed direct summand of
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
in
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
then (ii) provides morphisms

\alpha:\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T^{\prime}}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\hskip 11.38109pt\mbox{and }\beta:\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T^{\prime}}

which induce isomorphisms
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T^{\prime}}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T^{\prime}}^{(2)}\cong\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
. Now filtering by the ideals
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T^{\prime}}^{(n)}
and
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(n)}
shows that
\alpha
and
\beta
are inverse isomorphisms.

(iv) Because
T
has finite type, (cf §1.2)

\overline{U\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}}=\widehat{U\mathbb{L}_{T}}=\widehat{\bigotimes(T)}=\prod_{n}\bigotimes^{n}(T)

where
\bigotimes(T)
is the tensor algebra on
T
.

\square

Theorem 1
.

The following conditions are equivalent on a complete enriched Lie algebra,
L
.

(i)

L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is profree.

(ii)

The quadratic model
\land V
of
L
satisfies

H(\land V)=\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)

In this case division by
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
converts the isomorphism
s\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}V^{\vee}
to an isomorphism
sT\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee}
.

6.1
Quadratic Sullivan algebras

For any quadratic Sullivan algebra,
\land V
, recall (§4) that the standard filtration of
V
is given by

V_{0}=V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }V_{n+1}=V\cap d^{-1}(\land^{2}V_{n}).

In particular,

H(\land V)=\oplus_{k}H^{[k]}(\land V),

where
H^{[k]}(\land V)
is the image in
H(\land V)
of the space of cycles in
\land^{k}V
.
Our objective here is to prove (Lemma
6.2
) that

H^{[2]}(\land V)=0\Longleftrightarrow H(\land V)=\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d).

Lemma 6.1
.

Suppose
\psi:E\to L
is a morphism of complete enriched Lie algebras.

(i)

\psi
is surjective if and only if
\psi(2):E/E^{(2)}\to L/L^{(2)}
is surjective.

(ii)

Suppose the quadratic model,
\land V
, of
L
satisfies
H^{[2]}(\land V)=0
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(a)

\psi(2)
is an isomorphism,

(b)

\psi(n)
is an isomorphism,
n\geq 2
,

(c)

\psi
is an isomorphism.

proof.
First recall that
\psi
is the dual of
\varphi:V\to W
where
\varphi:\land V\to\land W
is the corresponding morphism between the quadratic models of
L
and
E
. In view of Lemma
4.1
,
\psi(n+2):E/E^{(n+2)}\to L/L^{(n+2)}
is the dual of
\varphi_{n}:V_{n}\to W_{n}
.

(i) We have only to show that if
\varphi_{0}
is injective then
\varphi
is injective. Assume by induction that
\varphi_{n}
is injective. if
v\in V_{n+1}
and
\varphi v=0
then since
dv\in\land^{2}V_{n}
and
\varphi_{n}
is injective it follows that
dv=0
. Thus
v\in V_{0}
and since
\varphi_{0}
is injective
v=0
.

(ii) Suppose (a) is satisfied and assume by induction that
\varphi_{n}:V_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}W_{n}
. If
w\in W_{n+1}
then
dw
is a cycle in
\land^{2}W_{n}
and so
dw=\varphi\Phi
where
\Phi
is a cycle in
\land^{2}V_{n}
. Because
H^{[2]}(\land V)=0
, it follows that
\Phi=dv
for some
v\in V_{n+1}
. Thus
d(w-\varphi v)=0
and so
w-\varphi(v)\in W_{0}
. By hypothesis,
w-\varphi v=\varphi v_{0}
for some
v_{0}\in V_{0}
, and so
W_{n+1}\subset\varphi(V_{n+1})
. On the other hand, by (i),
\varphi_{n+1}:V_{n+1}\to W_{n+1}
is injective. This proves that (a)
\Rightarrow
(b).

But then
\varphi=\varinjlim\varphi_{n}
and so
\varphi
is an isomorphism. Thus (b)
\Rightarrow
(c). Finally, it is immediate that if
\varphi
is an isomorphism then so is
\varphi_{0}
.
\square

Lemma 6.2
.

If a morphism
\rho:\land V\to\land Z
of quadratic Sullivan algebras restricts to a surjection
\rho_{0}:V_{0}\to Z_{0}
, and if
H^{[2]}(\land V)=0
, then

H^{[k]}(\land Z)=0,\hskip 8.53581ptk\geq 2.

proof.
The proof is in two Steps.

Step One. If, in addition,
\rho
is surjective then
H^{[k]}(\land Z)=0
,
k\geq 2
.

Here we first show that
H^{[2]}(\land Z)=0
. Since
\rho
is surjective, by
[
19
, Cor.3.4]

\rho
extends to a quasi-isomorphism

\rho:\land V\otimes\land U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\land Z

in which
\land V\otimes\land U
is a
\Lambda
-extension of
\land V
and
d:\land U\to V\otimes\land U
. Since
\rho
is surjective there is a quasi-isomorphism
\sigma:\land Z\to\land V\otimes\land U
satisfying
\rho\circ\sigma=id
. Because
d:\land U\to V\otimes\land U
and
\land V\otimes\land U
is a
\Lambda
-extension it is straightforward to verify that
\sigma
may be constructed so that
\sigma:Z\to V\otimes\land U
. In particular, the decomposition
\land V\otimes\land U=\oplus_{k}\land^{k}V\otimes\land U
induces a decomposition of its homology, and

\sigma:H^{[k]}(\land Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H^{[k]}(\land V\otimes\land U),\hskip 14.22636ptk\geq 0.

On the other hand, because
\land V\otimes\land U
is a
\Lambda
-extension,
\land U
is the increasing union of the subspaces
(\land U)_{q}
given by

(\land U)_{0}=\land U\cap d^{-1}(\land V\otimes 1)\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }(\land U)_{q+1}=\land U\cap d^{-1}(\land V\otimes(\land U)_{q}).

Thus

H^{[k]}(\land V\otimes\land U)=\varinjlim_{q}H^{[k]}(\land V\otimes(\land U)_{q}).

But the differential in the quotients
\land V\otimes((\land U)_{q+1}/(\land U)_{q})
is just
d\otimes id
, and it follows that
H^{[2]}(\land V\otimes\land U)=0
.

It remains to show that
H^{[k]}(\land Z)=0
,
k\geq 3
. Suppose
\Phi\in\land^{k}Z
is a cycle. There is then a sequence
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}
of elements in
Z
such that
dz_{1}=0
,
dz_{i+1}\in\land^{2}(z_{1},\dots,z_{i})
,
\Phi\in\land(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})
and such that division by
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}
maps
\Phi
to zero. When
r=1
,
\Phi=z_{1}^{k}
is a boundary. We use induction on
r
and on
k
to show that
\Phi
is a boundary.

Observe first that division by
z_{1}
gives a quadratic Sullivan algebra
(\land Z^{\prime},d^{\prime})
. By what we just proved,
H^{[2]}(\land Z^{\prime},d^{\prime})=0
. Thus by induction on
r
, the image of
\Phi
in
\land Z^{\prime}
is a boundary. It follows that for some
\Phi^{\prime}\in\land^{k-1}Z
,

\Phi-d(1\otimes\Phi^{\prime})=z_{1}\otimes\Phi^{\prime\prime},

with
\Phi^{\prime\prime}\in\land Z
. In particular, in
\land Z^{\prime}
,
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
is a
d^{\prime}
-cycle. By induction on
k
in
\land Z^{\prime}
,
\Phi^{\prime\prime}=d^{\prime}\Omega
for some
\Omega\in\land^{k-2}Z^{\prime}
. Therefore
\Phi^{\prime\prime}=d\Omega+z_{1}\otimes\Psi
for some
\Psi
.

If deg
\,z_{1}
is odd then
z_{1}\otimes\Phi^{\prime\prime}=d(-z_{1}\otimes\Omega)
, and so
\Phi
is a boundary. If deg
\,z_{1}
is even then, since
H^{[2]}(\land Z)=0
, we may choose
z_{2}
so
dz_{2}=z_{1}^{2}
. Division by
z_{2}
and
z_{1}^{2}
then gives a quasi-isomorphism
\land Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left((\land z_{1})/z_{1}^{2}\right)\otimes\land Z^{\prime\prime}
and the same argument as above shows that
\Phi
is a boundary.

Step Two. Completion of the proof of Lemma
6.2
.

We define a sequence of surjective morphisms

\land V=\land V(1)\twoheadrightarrow\land V(2)\twoheadrightarrow\dots\twoheadrightarrow\land V(p)\twoheadrightarrow\dots

such that
\rho
factors through each to yield morphisms

\rho(p):\land V(p)\twoheadrightarrow\land Z.

In fact, if
\rho(p)
has been defined let
\varphi_{p}:\land V(p)\twoheadrightarrow\land V(p+1)
be obtained by division by
V(p)_{0}\cap\mbox{ker}\rho(p).

Then the kernels of the surjections
\land V\to\land V(p)
form an increasing sequence of subspaces
K(p)\subset V
. Set
K=\cup_{p}K(p)
and let

\varphi:\land V\to\land W

be the surjection obtained by dividing
V
by
K
. Then
\varphi=\varinjlim_{p}\varphi_{p}
, and so
\varphi
and
\varphi_{0}
are surjective. Thus by Step One
H^{[k]}(\land W)=0
,
k\geq 2
.

Now by construction,
\rho
factors as

\land V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\to}}\land W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma}}{{\to}}\land Z.

Moreover,
\land W=\varinjlim_{p}\land V(p)
and so
W_{0}=\varinjlim_{p}V(p)_{0}
. Let
I(p)
be the image of
V(p)_{0}
in
V(p+1)_{0}
. Then also

W_{0}=\varinjlim_{p}I(p).

Thus by construction,
\gamma:W_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}Z_{0}
. Now Lemma
6.1
(ii) asserts that
\gamma
is an isomorphism, and hence

H^{[k]}(\land Z)=H^{[k]}(\land W)=0,\hskip 8.53581ptk\geq 2.

\square

6.2
Proof of Theorem
1

This is in two steps. We fix a closed summand,
T
, of
L^{(2)}
in
L
and we denote by
(\land V,d)
the quadratic model of
L
. Then it follows from Lemma
4.1
that the isomorphism
L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}V^{\vee}
restricts to an isomorphism
T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee}
.

Step One.
If
L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is profree then
H(\land V)=\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)
.

In this case, by adjoining additional variables construct an inclusion
\lambda:\land V\to\land W
of quadratic Sullivan algebras for which

V_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}W_{0}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }H^{[2]}(\land W)=0.

Dualizing
V\to W
gives a surjection
L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\leftarrow}}L_{W}
of complete enriched Lie algebras. Moreover, since
V_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}W_{0}
,
L_{W}=L_{W}^{(2)}\oplus T_{W}
where
T_{W}
is closed and

\rho_{T}:T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longleftarrow}}T_{W}.

Here both
\rho_{T}
and its inverse,
\sigma
, are coherent and so
\sigma
extends to a morphism
\varphi:L\to L_{W}
. Since
\rho\circ\varphi|_{T}=id|_{T}
it follows that
\rho\circ\varphi=id_{L}
. Now dualizing,
\varphi
, gives a morphism

\psi:\land V\leftarrow\land W

such that
\psi\circ\lambda=id
.

On the other hand, the inclusion
\lambda
defines a
\Lambda
-extension

\land V\otimes\land Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\land W,

in which, if
Z\neq 0
then some
z\in Z
satisfies
dz\in\land^{2}V
. Thus

d\psi z=\psi dz=dz.

Hence
\psi z-z
is a cycle in
W
. This gives

\psi z-z\in W_{0}=V_{0}

and so
z\in V
. This contradicts
z\in Z
and it follows that
Z=0
and
\psi
is an isomorphism inverse to
\lambda
. In particular
H^{[2]}(\land V)=0
and now Lemma
6.2
implies that
H(\land V)=\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d).

Step Two.
If
H(\land V)=\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)
then
L
is profree.

In this case, by Proposition
6.1
, the inclusion
T\to L
extends to a morphism

\varphi:\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\to L.

On the one hand, Proposition
4.2
implies that the image of
\varphi
is closed. On the other hand, since the image contains
T
, Lemma
3.2
implies that
\varphi
is surjective. And, finally, Proposition
4.2
also asserts that ker
\,\varphi
is closed.

Now (§4.1), taking the quadratic models for the short exact sequence

0\leftarrow L\leftarrow\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\leftarrow\mbox{ker}\,\varphi\leftarrow 0,

yields the
\Lambda
-extension

\land V\to\land V\otimes\land Z\to\land Z

in which
\land V\otimes\land Z
is the quadratic model of
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
. We show that
\varphi
is an isomorphism by proving that
Z=0
.

But if
Z\neq 0
then for some
z\neq 0
in
Z
,
dz\in\land^{2}V
. Since by hypothesis
H^{[2]}(\land V)=0
this implies that
dz=dv
some
v\in V
. But then
v-z
is a cycle representing a class in
H^{[1]}(\land V\otimes\land Z)
. However, because
\varphi
induces an isomorphism
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}\to L/L^{(2)}
it follows that

V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d=H^{[1]}(\land V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H^{[1]}(\land V\otimes\land Z)=(V\oplus Z)\cap\mbox{ker}\,d.

Thus
z=0
and therefore
Z=0
.

\square

6.3
Properties of profree Lie algebras

We have from Theorem
1
:

Corollary 1.

Suppose
E=R\oplus E^{2}
is a free graded Lie algebra. If dim
\,R<\infty
and
E
is equipped with the unique (Proposition
3.3
) enriched structure then
\overline{E}
is profree.

proof.
By Proposition
3.1
,
\overline{E}=R\oplus\overline{E}^{(2)}
. Now apply Theorem
1
.

\square

Corollary 2.

Suppose
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is a profree Lie algebra. Then

(i)

If
S\subset T
is any closed subspace then the closure
\overline{E}
of the sub Lie algebra
E
generated by
S
satisfies

\overline{E}=S\oplus\overline{E}^{(2)},

and
\overline{E}^{(2)}=\overline{E}\cap L^{(2)}
.

(ii)

If
0\neq E
is a solvable sub Lie algebra of
L
, then
E
is a free Lie algebra on a single generator.

proof.
(i) By Lemma
3.2
,
\overline{E}^{(2)}=\overline{E^{2}}\subset L^{(2)}
. Therefore
E=S\oplus\overline{E}^{(2)}
. Since
\overline{E}
is profree,
S
generates a free Lie algebra.

(ii) Denote by
E\supset\dots\supset E^{[k]}\supset\dots
the sequence of ideals defined by
E^{[k+1]}=[E^{[k]},E^{[k]}]
. By hypotheses, some
E^{[n+1]}=0
. Then, by induction on
n
, we may assume
[E,E]
is either zero or a free Lie algebra on a single generator. It follows that the closure,
\overline{E}\subset L
satisfies dim
\,\overline{E}^{(2)}\leq 2
, since (Lemma
3.2
)
\overline{E}^{(2)}=\overline{E^{2}}
.

But since
E\neq 0
, Corollary 1 gives that
\overline{E}=S\oplus\overline{E}^{(2)}
and
S
generates a free Lie algebra. This implies that
\overline{E}
is the free Lie algebra generated by a single element. In particular dim
\,\overline{E}\leq 2
and so
E=\overline{E}
.

\square

Corollary 3.

If
E
is a complete enriched Lie algebra then the inclusion of a direct summand
T
of
E^{(2)}
in
E
extends to a surjection
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\to E
.

proof.
This follows by the same argument as at the start of the proof of Step Two in the Theorem
1
.

\square

Corollary 4.

If
\psi:(L,{\mathcal{I}})\to(F,{\mathcal{G}})
is a surjective morphism from a complete enriched Lie algebra to a profree Lie algebra then there is a morphism

\sigma:(F,{\mathcal{G}})\to(L,{\mathcal{I}})

such that
\psi\circ\sigma=id_{F}
.

proof.
Let
\varphi:\land V_{F}\to\land V
be the injective morphism of the corresponding quadratic models which dualizes to
\psi
. Write
V=\varphi(V_{F})\oplus S
. Inverting
\varphi
then defines an isomorphism
\gamma
from the sub quadratic algebra
\land\varphi(F)
to
\land V_{F}
and clearly
\gamma\circ\varphi=id
. It remains to extend
\gamma
to
S
so that
\gamma(dw)=d\gamma(w)
,
w\in S
.

For this set
\gamma=0
in
S\cap V_{0}
. Now suppose
\gamma
has been defined in
S\cap V_{n}
and let
\{z_{i}\}
be a basis of a direct summand of
S\cap V_{n}
in
S\cap V_{n+1}
. Then
\gamma(dz_{i})
is a cycle in
\land^{2}V_{F}
. Hence
\gamma(dz_{i})=dw_{i}
for some
w_{i}\in V_{F}
. Extend
\gamma
by setting
\gamma z_{i}=w_{i}
.
\square

Now recall from
[
19
, Chap 9]
that the category of a minimal Sullivan algebra,
\land V
, is the least
p
(or
\infty
) such that
\land V
is a homotopy retract of
\land V/\land^{>p}V
.

Proposition 6.2
.

(i) A complete enriched Lie algebra is profree if and only if its quadratic model satisfies cat
(\land V)=1
.

(ii) Any closed sub Lie algebra of a profree Lie algebra is profree.

proof.
(i) The condition cat
(\land V)=1
for a quadratic Sullivan algebra is equivalent to the condition
\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\land V
. Thus (i) follows from Theorem
1
.

(ii) Suppose
\land V\to\land Z
is the morphism of quadratic models corresponding to an inclusion
E\to L
of a closed sub Lie algebra in a complete enriched Lie algebra. Then
[
19
, Theorem 9.3]
gives cat
(\land Z)\leq
cat
(\land V)
. Thus (ii) follows from (i).

\square

Remark.
Recall that any sub Lie algebra of a free Lie algebra is free (
[
42
]
). By Proposition
6.2
(ii) the analogous statement for complete Lie algebras and closed sub algebras is also true.

Example.

Let
L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
be a profree Lie algebra. Then the sub Lie algebra generated by
T
is free. The closed sub Lie algebras of
L
are profree, but the sub Lie algebras are not necessarily free. For instance let
L=\widehat{\mathbb{L}(a,b)}
and
E\subset L
be the sub Lie algebra generated by the elements
a,b
and
\omega=\sum_{n\geq 1}\mbox{ad}^{n}_{a}(b)
. Since
\omega=[a,\omega]-[a,b]
,
\omega\in E^{2}
, so that
E/E^{2}
is generated by the classes of
a
and
b
. If
E
was free, then
E
would be equal to
\mathbb{L}(a,b)
, which is impossible because
\omega\not\in\mathbb{L}(a,b)
.

6.4
The structure of a profree Lie algebra

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
L
be a complete enriched Lie algebra.

(i)

Suppose
L/[L,L]
has finite type. Then
L
is profree if and only if the inclusion
T\to L
of a direct summand of
[L,L]
extends to an isomorphism

\widehat{\mathbb{L}_{T}}:=\varprojlim_{n}{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}L.

(ii)

If
L=\varprojlim_{\sigma}L_{\sigma}
is any inverse limit of profree Lie algebras then
L
is profree.

proof.
(i). Proposition
3.2
identifies
\widehat{\mathbb{L}_{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
, since
\mathbb{L}_{T}
has a unique enriched structure. Thus (i) follows from Theorem
1
.

(ii) If
\land V
and
\land V_{\sigma}
are respectively the quadratic models of
L
and
L_{\sigma}
then

\land V=\varinjlim_{\sigma}\land V_{\sigma}
and the map
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d\to\varinjlim_{\sigma}V_{\sigma}\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
is an isomorphism. It follows (since each
L_{\sigma}
is profree) from Theorem
1
that
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{\geq 1}(\land V)
, and hence (Theorem
1
) that
L
is profree.

\square

Now, suppose that
\land V
is the quadratic model of a profree Lie algebra,
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
. In particular

\mathbb{L}_{T}=\oplus_{k\geq 1}T(k),

where
T(k)
is the linear span of the iterated Lie brackets of length
k
in elements of
T
. In general, however the subspaces
T(k)
may not be closed. Nevertheless we do have an analogous structure for
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
.

For this, denote the enriched structure of
T
by
\{\rho_{\sigma}:T\to T(\sigma)\}
. Then by definition,

\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}=\varprojlim_{\sigma}\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{L}_{T(\sigma)}/\mathbb{L}^{n}_{T(\sigma)}=\varprojlim_{\sigma}L(\sigma),

where
L(\sigma)=\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{T(\sigma)}
. Then write
\mathbb{L}_{T}=\oplus_{k\geq 1}T(k)
, where
T(k)
is the span of the iterated Lie brackets of length

k
in elements of
T
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
be a profree Lie algebra. Then, with the notation above,

(i)

L^{(k)}=\varprojlim_{\sigma}L(\sigma)^{k}
.

(ii)

L^{(k)}=\overline{T(k)}\oplus L^{(k+1)}
.

(iii)

For each
k,\ell
,
[\overline{T(k)},\overline{T(\ell)}]\subset\overline{T(k+\ell)}
. In particular,
\oplus_{k}\overline{T(k)}
is a weighted Lie algebra with closure
L=\prod_{k}\overline{T(k)}
.

proof.
(i) Since
L=\varprojlim_{\sigma}L(\sigma)
it follow that
V=\varinjlim_{\sigma}V_{\sigma}
where
\land V_{\sigma}
is the quadratic model of
L(\sigma)
. This gives

V/V_{k}=\varinjlim_{\sigma}V(\sigma)/V(\sigma)_{k},

and (Lemma
4.1
) this dualizes to
L^{(k)}=\varprojlim L(\sigma)^{(k)}
. Since
L(\sigma)=\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{T(\sigma)}
we have
L(\sigma)^{(k)}=L(\sigma)^{k}
.

(ii) By definition, the enriched structure in
T(k)
is provided by the surjections
{\rho_{\sigma}}|_{T(k)}
. But since
\rho_{\sigma}:T\to T(\sigma)
, it restricts to surjections

\textstyle{\rho_{\sigma}:T(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T(\sigma,k),}

where
T(\sigma,k)
is the span of the iterated Lie brackets of length
k
in elements of
T(\sigma)
. This gives

\overline{T(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varprojlim_{\sigma}T(\sigma,k).

On the other hand the inclusion of
T(k)
in
\mathbb{L}_{T}^{k}
induces a map
\overline{T(k)}\to L^{(k)}/L^{(k+1)}
.
But this factors as

\overline{T(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varprojlim_{\sigma}T(k,\sigma)=\varprojlim_{\sigma}L(\sigma)^{(k)}/L(\sigma)^{k+1)}=L^{(k)}/L^{(k+1)}.

Thus this is an isomorphism and
L^{(k)}=\overline{T(k)}\oplus L^{(k+1)}.

(iii) This is immediate from
[T(k),T(\ell)]\subset T(k+\ell)
.

\square

The next Proposition explains the difference between
L^{2}
and
L^{(2)}
for general profree Lie algebras.

Proposition 6.5
.

If
L=L_{0}
is a profree Lie algebra and dim
\,L/[L,L]=\infty
, then
L^{2}\neq L^{(2)}
. If in particular,
L/L^{(2)}
is concentrated in degree
r
, then
L^{2}_{2r}\neq L^{(2)}_{2r}
.

proof.

Indeed, let
(\land W,d)
be the quadratic model of
L
. We can decompose
W
as an union
W=\cup_{n}W_{n}
with
W_{0}=W\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
, and for
n>1
,
W_{n}=d^{-1}(\land^{2}W_{n-1})
. We denote by
Z_{n}
a direct summand of
W_{n-1}
in
W_{n}
. Then by hypothesis
W_{0}
is infinite dimensional and there is a quasi-isomorphism
\varphi:(\land W,d)\to(\mathbb{Q}\oplus W_{0},0)
that is the identity on
W_{0}
and that maps each
Z_{n}
to
0
.

For sake of simplicity, we write
V=W_{0}
and
Z=Z_{1}
. By construction the isomorphism
L=(sW)^{\vee}
induces isomorphisms

L/L^{(2)}\cong(sV)^{\vee},\hskip 28.45274pt\mbox{and }L^{(2)}/L^{(3)}\cong(sZ)^{\vee}.

Suppose first
W
countable and denote by
w_{1},w_{2},\dots
a basis of
V
. Since
d=0
on
V
,
d:Z\to\land^{2}V
is an isomorphism. We denote by
w_{ij}
,
i<j
the basis of
Z
defined by
d(w_{ij})=w_{i}\land w_{j}
. Denote by
E
the vector space of column matrices
X=(x_{i})
with only a finite number of nonzero
a_{i}
. Then the map
(a_{i})\leadsto\sum a_{i}w_{i}
defines an isomorphism
E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}V
.

Let represent an element
\varphi\in Z^{\vee}
by the infinite dimensional antisymmetric matrix
M_{\varphi}
,

(M_{\varphi})_{ij}=\varphi(w_{ij}),\hskip 14.22636pti<j.

In a similar process, an element
f\in V^{\vee}
can be represented by a column matrix
A_{f}
, with
(A_{f})_{i}=f(w_{i})
. The vector space ker
\,f
can then be identified with the sub vector space of
E
formed by the column matrices
X
satisfying
A_{f}^{t}\cdot X=0
. (Here
A^{t}
denotes the line matrix transposition of a column matrix
A
.)

Note that when we have two column matrices
A
and
B
, we can form the antisymmetric matrix
A\cdot B^{t}-B\cdot A^{t}
. Now remark that for
f,g\in V^{\vee}
, we have

M_{[f,g]}=A_{f}\cdot B_{g}^{t}-B_{g}\cdot A_{f}^{t}.

Let
\varphi_{0}\in Z^{\vee}
be the particular element defined for
i<j
by

\varphi_{0}(w_{ij})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if $w_{ij}=w_{2k+1,2k+2}$, for some $k$}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The element
\varphi_{0}
can be extended to all of
W
, by
\varphi_{0}(V)=0
and
\varphi_{0}(Z_{n})=0
, for
n>1
. By construction
\varphi_{0}\in L^{(2)}
.
The associated matrix is

M_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}B_{0}&0&0&\dots\\
0&B_{0}&0&\dots\\
0&0&B_{0}&\dots\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\end{array}\right)\hskip 42.67912pt\mbox{with }B_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right).

Now consider a finite sum
\sum_{i=1}^{n}[f_{i},g_{i}]
, with
f_{i}
and
g_{i}\in V^{\vee}
. The associated matrix is
\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{[f_{i},g_{i}]}
. Then

K=(\cap_{i=1}^{n}\mbox{ker}\,f_{i})\cap(\cap_{i=1}^{n}\mbox{ker}\,g_{i})

is infinite dimensional, and so for some non-zero
X\in Z^{\vee}
,
(\sum M_{[f_{i},g_{i}]})\cdot X=0
. Since
M_{0}\cdot X\neq 0
, it follows that
L^{2}\subset_{\neq}L^{(2)}
.

In the general case, let
E\subset V
be a countable subvector space and let
\land T
be the minimal model of
(\mathbb{Q}\oplus E,0)
. Then its homotopy Lie algebra
L_{T}
is a retract of
L
,

\textstyle{L_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\rho}

Let
\varphi_{0}\in L_{T}^{(2)}
, not in
L_{T}^{2}
.Then
j(\varphi_{0})\in L^{(2)}
and not in
L^{2}
because otherwise
\varphi_{0}=\rho(\varphi)
would belong to
L_{T}^{2}
.

\square

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
be a profree Lie algebra in which
T=T_{0}
and
sT_{0}
is the dual of a vector space of countably infinite dimension. Then
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is not pronilpotent.

proof.
Denote the quadratic Sullivan model of
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
by
(\land S,d)
. Then (Theorem
1
)

sT_{0}=(S\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee},\hskip 8.53581pt\mathbb{Q}\oplus(S\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H(\land S),\hskip 8.53581pt\mbox{and }s\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}=S^{\vee}.

Thus
S\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
has a basis of the form
v_{i},w_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
. This in turn determines elements
x_{i},y_{i}\in T_{0}
by the conditions

<v_{i},sx_{j}>=\delta_{ij}=<w_{i},sy_{j}>\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }<v_{i},sy_{j}>=0=<w_{i},sx_{j}>.

Now define
\omega\in\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
by

\omega=\sum_{n\geq 1}ad^{n}_{x_{n}}y_{n}.

We shall show by construction that
\omega\not\in\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{2}
and then use Lemma
3.1
(iii) to prove that
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is not pronilpotent.

Suppose then that
\omega\in\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{2}
, and write

\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{N}[\omega^{\prime}_{i},\omega_{i}^{\prime\prime}].

Since
sT=S^{\vee}
the elements of
T
are linear functions
S\to\mathbb{Q}
of degree
-1
. Thus

K=(\cap\mbox{ker}\,\omega_{i}^{\prime})\cap(\cap\mbox{ker}\,\omega_{i}^{\prime\prime})

is a subspace of
S
of finite codimension. Thus for some non-zero linear combination,

a=\sum_{i=p}^{p+q}\lambda_{i}v_{i},

we have
a\in K
. We may, and do, assume that
\lambda_{p}=1
.

Now define a sequence
(a_{r})_{r\geq 1}
of elements of
S
by the conditions

da_{1}=aw_{p},\hskip 8.53581ptda_{r}=aa_{r-1}\hskip 5.69054pt\mbox{if }r\geq 2,\hskip 8.53581pt\mbox{and }<a_{r},sT_{0}>=0,r\geq 1.

It then follows from (
4
) that since
<a,s\omega_{i}^{\prime}>=0=<a,s\omega_{i}^{\prime\prime}>
,

<a_{r},s\omega>=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,<da_{r},s\omega_{i}^{\prime},s\omega_{i}^{\prime\prime}>=0,\hskip 8.53581ptr\geq 1.

On the other hand, an iterated induction argument using (6) shows that

<a_{p},s\,ad_{x_{k}}^{k}y_{k}>=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }k=p\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

It follows that
<a_{p},s\omega>=1
, which is the desired contradiction.

Finally, set

z_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}ad_{x_{n}}^{k}y_{k}.

Then
z_{n+1}-z_{n}\in\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{n+1}
. Thus the sequence
(z_{n})
is a coherent sequence in the tower

\dots\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{n+1}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{n}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{n-1}\to\dots

and therefore defines an element
z\in\widehat{\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}}=\varprojlim_{n}\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{n}
. Now Lemma
3.1
(iii) provides a retraction
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\rightarrow}}\widehat{\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi}}{{\rightarrow}}\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
in which
\psi z=\omega
. Since
\psi\circ\varphi=id
,
\psi(\varphi\omega)=\omega
. Thus if
\psi
is an isomorphism,
z=\omega
and the images of
z
and
\omega
in
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{2}
coincide. But the image
z
is in
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{2}
and
\omega\not\in\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{2}
.
\square

Part III
Topological spaces, Sullivan algebras, and homotopy Lie algebras

Recall that we identify the categories of topological spaces and simplicial sets via the singular simplex and Milnor realization functors (
[
35
]
).

7
Sullivan models: spatial realizations and Sullivan completions

Sullivan’s approach to rational homotopy theory is based on a two-way relation between topological spaces and his Sullivan algebras (
[
44
]
,
[
19
, §1.6]
), which we briefly review for the convenience of the reader. This relation is obtained from two contravariant adjoint functors

A_{PL}
: simplicial sets
\leadsto
cdga’s
and

\langle\,,\,\rangle
: cdga’s
\leadsto
simplicial sets.

These are constructed as follows from a simplicial cdga
A_{PL}=\{(A_{PL})_{n}\}
of acyclic algebras:

A_{PL}(X)=Simpl(A,A_{PL})\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }\langle A\rangle=Cdga(A,A_{PL}).

Here the cdga structure of
A_{PL}(X)
and the simplicial structure of
\langle A\rangle
are inherited from the corresponding structures of
A_{PL}
. The adjointness of these functors follows from the equalities for all cdga’s
A
and simplicial sets
X
:

\displaystyle Cdga(A,A_{PL}(X))=\mbox{Hom}(A\times X,A_{PL})=Simpl(X,\langle A\rangle).

(12)

In particular, adjoint pairs will be denoted by

\varphi:A\to A_{PL}(X)\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }|\varphi|:X\to\langle A\rangle.

Now specialize this construction from all cdga’s to the category of Sullivan algebras,
\land V
. (For simplicity we denote
(\land V,d)
simply by
\land V
when we do not need to specify the differential.)

There is a natural equivalence relation (
homotopy
) among cdga morphisms from a minimal Sullivan algebra (
[
44
]
,
[
19
, §1.7]
). It is straightforward from the definitions that (
12
) induces a natural bijection

\displaystyle[\land V,A_{PL}(X)]\cong[X,\langle\land V\rangle]

(13)

of based homotopy classes of morphisms.

In particular, the two-way relation between topological spaces and Sullivan algebras is defined by the

Definition.

(i)

A
(minimal) Sullivan model
of a connected space
X
is a quasi-isomorphism

\varphi:\land V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{PL}(X)

from a (minimal) Sullivan algebra.

(ii)

The
spatial realization
of a Sullivan algebra,
\land V
, is the connected based simplicial set
\langle\land V\rangle
.

Remark.
The uniqueness (up to homotopy class of quasi-isomorphism) of a Sullivan model implies the uniqueness (up to homotopy class of homotopy equivalence) of a Sullivan completion.

Now observe that any minimal Sullivan algebra,
\land V
, computes
\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle
via a natural bijection

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle\cong V^{\vee}.

(14)

In fact, if
\land W(n)
is the minimal model of the
n
-sphere
S^{n}
we have natural cdga quasi-isomorphisms

\textstyle{A_{PL}(S^{n})}
\textstyle{\land W(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{H(S^{n})=\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}a_{n},}

a_{n}
denoting the orientation class of
S^{n}
. The bijections (
13
) convert these to bijections

\pi_{n}\langle\land V\rangle=[S^{n},\langle\land V\rangle]=[\land V,H(S^{n})]=(V^{n})^{\vee}.

These bijections naturally endow
\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle
with the structure of a graded rational vector space. (Moreover (
[
19
, Theorem 1.4]
) for
n\geq 2
, they are isomorphisms of abelian groups).

To summarize:

(i)

If
\varphi:\land V\to A_{PL}(X)
is a Sullivan model then
\land V
computes the rational cohomology algebra
H(X)
via

H(\varphi):H(\land V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}H(X).

(ii)

If
\land V
is any minimal Sullivan algebra then
\land V
computes
\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle
via the natural bijection

\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle\cong V^{\vee}.

(iii)

If
\varphi:\land V\to A_{PL}(X)
is a minimal Sullivan algebra then the adjoint
|\varphi|:X\to\langle\land V\rangle
induces

\pi_{*}(|\varphi|):\pi_{*}(X)\to\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle

connecting
\pi_{*}(X)
to
\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle
.

A more general connection between Sullivan algebras
\land V
and its spatial realization
\langle\land V\rangle
is provided by the morphism

\iota_{\land V}:\land V\to A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle

adjoint to
id_{\langle\land V\rangle}
. In fact, if
\psi:\land V\to A_{PL}(X)
is a morphism from an arbitrary Sullivan algebra then it is easy to verify that

\displaystyle\psi=A_{PL}(|\psi|)\circ\iota_{\land V}.

(15)

We also note that immediately from the definitions we obtain

Proposition 7.1
.

Suppose
\land V
and
\land W
are Sullivan algebras. Then

(i)

The natural map

\langle\land V\otimes\land W\rangle\to\langle\land V\rangle\times\langle\land W\rangle

is a homotopy equivalence

(ii)

If
\land Z
is the minimal Sullivan model of
\land V\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land W
then the natural map

\langle\land Z\rangle\to\langle\land V\rangle\vee\langle\land W\rangle

is a homotopy equivalence.

(iii)

The diagram

\textstyle{\land V\otimes\land W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle\otimes A_{PL}\langle\land W\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(\langle\land V\rangle\times\langle\land W\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\land V\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle\times_{\mathbb{Q}}A_{PL}\langle\land W\rangle}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(\langle\land V\rangle\vee\langle\land W\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

commutes. If either
H(\langle\land V\rangle)
or
H(\langle\land W\rangle)
is a graded vector space of finite type then
A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle\otimes A_{PL}\langle\land W\rangle\longleftarrow A_{PL}(\langle\land V\rangle\times\langle\land W\rangle)
is also a quasi-isomorphism.

8
The homotopy Lie algebra of a minimal Sullivan algebra

Associated with a minimal Sullivan algebra,
(\land V,d)
, is the quadratic Sullivan algebra,
(\land V,d_{1})
defined by:
d_{1}
is the component in
\land^{2}V
of
dv
. This in turn determines (Proposition
4.1
) the complete enriched Lie algebra
L_{V}=(L_{V})_{\geq 0}
given by

sL_{V}=V^{\vee}\hskip 11.38109pt\mbox{(as a graded vector space)}

and

<v,s[x,y]>=(-1)^{1+deg\,y}<d_{1}v,sx,sy>.

Definition.

L_{V}
is the
homotopy Lie algebra
of
(\land V,d)
.

Remarks.
1. Filtering by the ideals
\land^{\geq p}V
yields a spectral sequence, natural with respect to morphisms of minimal Sullivan algebras, and converging from
(\land V,d_{1})
to
H(\land V,d)
.

2. If
\land V,d)=\varinjlim_{\sigma}(\land V_{\sigma},d)
then
L_{V}=\varprojlim_{\sigma}L_{V_{\sigma}}
.

3. The enriched structure
L_{V}=\varprojlim_{\alpha}L_{V_{\alpha}}
enables the extension of (
[
19
, Chapter 2]
) to all homotopy Lie algebras, and with the same proofs. This removes the restriction dim
\,H^{1}(\land V)<\infty
, which is assumed in
[
19
]
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Suppose
\varphi,\psi:(\land V,d)\to(\land W,d)
are homotopic morphisms between minimal Sullivan algebras. Then

(i)

The induced morphisms
\varphi_{1},\psi_{1}:(\land V,d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{1})
coincide.

(ii)

The induced morphisms
L_{V}\leftarrow L_{W}
coincide.

(iii)

\varphi|_{V^{1}}=\psi|_{V^{1}}.

proof.
(i) Suppose
\Phi:(\land V,d)\to(\land W,d)\otimes\land(t,dt)
is a homotopy from
\varphi
to
\psi
and denote by
(\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}):\land W\otimes\land(t,dt)\to\land W\times\land W
the morphism sending
t
to
0,1
. Then let
U=\mbox{ker}\,\varepsilon_{0}\cap\mbox{ker}\,\varepsilon_{1}
and
A=\land^{\geq 1}W\otimes\land(t,dt)\oplus\mathbb{Q}
. This yields the quasi-isomorphisms

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{A\otimes\land U\otimes\land dU\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\land W\otimes\land(t,dt)}

in which
d:U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}dU
,
\rho(U)=0
, and
\gamma|_{U}
is the inclusion.

Now lift
\Phi
through
\gamma
and then compose with
\rho
to obtain a morphism

\Psi:\land V\to A.

Composing
\Psi
with the inclusion
j:A\to\land W\otimes\land(t,dt)
gives a second homotopy, and because of the choice of
U
it is also a homotopy from
\varphi
to
\psi
. Now filtering by the ideals
\land^{\geq j}V
and
\land^{\geq j}W
provides a homotopy

\Gamma:(\land V,d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{1})\otimes\land(t,dt)

from
\varphi_{1}
to
\psi_{1}
. Moreover, division by
\land^{\geq 2}V
and
\land^{\geq 2}W
converts
\Gamma
into a chain homotopy between

\varphi_{1}|_{V},\psi_{1}|_{V}:(V,0)\to(W,0).

It follows that
\varphi_{1}|_{V}=\psi_{1}|_{V}
and therefore that
\varphi_{1}=\psi_{1}
.

(ii) This is immediate from (i).

(iii) This follows because
\varphi|_{V^{1}}=\varphi_{1}|_{V^{1}}=\psi_{1}|_{V^{1}}=\psi|_{V^{1}}
.
\square

Now, for any minimal Sullivan algebra
\land V
the identifications (
14
) extend to natural bijections

\displaystyle\pi_{n}\langle\land V\rangle=(V^{n})^{\vee}=s(L_{V})_{n-1}.

(16)

This converts the Lie brackets in
L_{V}
to the Whitehead products in
\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle
:

Proposition 8.2
.

Suppose
(\land V,d)
is a minimal Sullivan algebra. Then

(i)

For
n\geq 2
, (
16
) is a linear isomorphism. In particular,
\pi_{n}\langle\land V\rangle
is the rational vector space,
s(L_{V})_{n-1}
.

(ii)

For
p,q\geq 2
, (
16
) identifies the Lie bracket
(L_{V})_{p}\times(L_{V})_{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\,,\,]}}{{\longrightarrow}}(L_{V})_{p+q}
with the Whitehead product
\pi_{p+1}\langle\land V\rangle\times\,\pi_{q+1}\langle\land V\rangle\to\pi_{p+q+1}\langle\land V\rangle
.

(iii)

The composite

\textstyle{G_{L}}
\textstyle{(L_{V})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\exp}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

is an isomorphism of groups, where
G_{L}
is the fundamental group of
L_{V}
defined in §1.2.

(iv)

Finally (
16
) identifies the right adjoint representation of
\pi_{1}\langle\land V\rangle
in
\pi_{n}\langle\land V\rangle
as

\beta\bullet\exp\alpha=e^{-ad\,\alpha}(\beta),\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\in(L_{V})_{0},\beta\in L_{V}.

proof.
Recall that
(\land V,d)=\varinjlim_{\alpha}(\land V_{\alpha},d)
in which the
V_{\alpha}
are finite dimensional subspaces of
V
. It follows that
\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle=\varprojlim_{\alpha}\langle\land V_{\alpha}\rangle
. Now Proposition
8.2
follows respectively from
[
19
, Theorem 1.4]
,
[
17
, Proposition 13.16]
,
[
19
, Theorem 2.4]
and
[
19
, Theorem 2.5]
.

\square

To summarize, a Sullivan algebra
\land V
computes
\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle
together with its Whitehead products, the group structure in
\pi_{1}\langle\land V\rangle
, and the action of
\pi_{1}\langle\land V\rangle
on
\pi_{\geq 2}\langle\land V\rangle
.

Example.
Denote by
\land V
the minimal Sullivan model of
S^{1}\vee S^{3}
. Then
V^{1}=\mathbb{Q}v
while
V^{3}
has a basis
w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{n},\dots
satisfying
dw_{0}=0
and
dw_{n+1}=vw_{n}
. Define
x\in(L_{V})_{0}
by
<v,sx>=1
and identifies
\pi_{3}\langle\land V\rangle=(V^{3})^{\vee}
as the space of infinite series

g(t)=\sum_{n\geq 0}c_{n}t^{n}

with
<w_{n},g(t)>=c_{n}
. Then the action of
\pi_{1}\langle\land V\rangle
in
\pi_{3}\langle\land V\rangle
is given by

g(t)\bullet\exp x=e^{t}\cdot g(t).

Finally, the connection between the properties of
H(\land V)
and
L_{V}
is illustrated in

Proposition 8.3
.

The following conditions on a minimal Sullivan algebra
(\land V,d)
are equivalent:

(i)

L_{V}=\varprojlim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L(n)
in which each
L(n)
is a finite dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra.

(ii)

dim
\,V
is at most countable.

(iii)

dim
\,H(\land V,d)
is at most countable.

proof.
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii). Let
\land V_{n}
be the quadratic Sullivan model of
L(n)
. Then
(\land V,d_{1})=\varinjlim_{n}(\land V_{n},d_{1})
is at most countable. This proves (ii) and (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) is obvious.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Define subspaces
W_{n}\subset V
by
W_{0}=V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
and
W_{n+1}=V\cap d^{-1}(\land W_{n})
. It follows from (iii) that each dim
\,W_{n}
is at most countable. But because
(\land V,d)
is a Sullivan algebra it follows that
V=\cup_{n}W_{n}
. and so dim
\,V
is at most countable. Thus because
(\land V,d)
is a Sullivan algebra
V=\cup_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}
where dim
\,V_{\alpha}<\infty
and
\land V_{\alpha}
is preserved by
d
. Since dim
\,V
is at most countable this implies that
V=\varinjlim_{n}V_{n}
where
(\land V_{n},d)
is a sub Sullivan algebra and dim
\,V_{n}<\infty
; Thus
L_{V}=\varprojlim_{n}L(n)
,
L(n)
denoting the homotopy Lie algebra of
(\land V_{n},d)
.

\square

Corollary.
If
(\land V,d)
is the minimal Sullivan of a space
X
and satisfies the conditions of the Proposition then
H(X)
is a graded vector space of finite type.

8.1
Profree homotopy Lie algebras

Profree Lie algebras are characterized in Part II in terms of their quadratic Sullivan models. This can, however, be extended to general minimal Sullivan algebras (Proposition
8.4
below), and they arise, with their sub Lie algebras, in a natural way as homotopy Lie algebras of wedges of spheres.

Proposition 8.4
.

Denote by
L
and by
(\land V,d)
the homotopy Lie algebra and associated quadratic Sullivan algebra of a minimal Sullivan algebra
(\land V,d)
. The following conditions are then equivalent

(i)

There is a quasi-isomorphism
(\land V,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathbb{Q}\oplus S
with zero differential in
S
and
S\cdot S=0
.

(ii)

The generating space
V
can be chosen so that
d:V\to\land^{2}V
and

\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}H(\land V,d).

(iii)

\mathbb{Q}\oplus V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H(\land V,d_{1})
.

(iv)

L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is profree, with
sT=H^{\geq 1}(\land V)^{\vee}=S^{\vee}
defining the enriched structure.

If they hold then
(\land V,d)\cong(\land V,d_{1})
and, if
V
is chosen to satisfy (iii), then
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d=V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}
.

proof.
(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii). First, let
S=S^{\geq 1}
be any graded vector space. Then
\mathbb{Q}\oplus S
, with zero differential and
S\cdot S=0
is a cdga, and its minimal model has the form

(\land V,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathbb{Q}\oplus S,0).

On the other hand, a successive adjoining of variables
w
to
S
constructs a quadratic Sullivan algebra
(\land W,d_{1})
such that
H^{[1]}(\land W,d_{1})=S
and
H^{[2]}(\land W)=0
. (Here as in Part II,
H^{[k]}(\land W,d_{1})
denotes the homology classes represented by cycles in
\land^{k}W
.) Now Lemma
6.2
asserts that
H^{[k]}(\land W)=0
,
k\geq 2
. Thus division by
\land^{\geq 2}W
and by a direct summand of
S
in
W
defines a quasi-isomorphism

(\land W,d_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathbb{Q}\oplus S.

Since minimal models are unique it follows that there is an isomorphism

(\land W,d_{1})\cong(\land V,d).

Now choose
V
so this isomorphism takes
W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}V
.

Now, if (ii) holds, then with the given choice of
V
, division by
\land^{\geq 2}V
and by a direct summand of
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
in
V
defines a quasi-isomorphism

(\land V,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d).

This gives (i).

(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii). If (i) holds,
the observations above yield an isomorphism
(\land W,d_{1})\cong(\land V,d)
. This induces an isomorphism
(\land W,d_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}(\land V,d_{1})
, which for appropriate choice of
V
preserves wedge degree. It follows that with this choice
(\land V,d)
is quadratic and
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d=V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}
. This proves (iii), and the same argument shows that (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii).

Finally, the assertion (iii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv) is Theorem
1
.
\square

Corollary 1.
If
H^{\geq 1}(X)\neq 0
then the homotopy Lie algebra
L_{X}
is profree if and only if
X
is formal and
H^{\geq 1}(X)\cdot H^{\geq 1}(X)\neq 0
. (A space
X
is
formal
if its minimal Sullivan model is also a minimal Sullivan model of
(H(X),0)
.)

Corollary 2.
If cat
\,X=1
then the homotopy Lie algebra,
L_{X}
is profree.

proof.
It follows from (
[
19
, Formula (9.1) and Theorem 9.1]
) that if cat
\,X=1
then its minimal Sullivan model satisfies condition (i) of the Proposition.
\square

Corollary 3.
If a connected co-H-space,
X
, satisfies
H^{\geq 1}(X)\neq 0
, then
L_{X}
is profree.

proof.
Let
U
be a contractible open neighbourhood of a base point in
X
. Then
X\vee U
and
U\vee X
form an open cover for
X\vee X

Since X is a co-H-space, the diagonal map
\Delta:X\to X\times X
factors up to homotopy through
X\vee X
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\vee X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\times X.}

The open sets
f^{-1}(X\vee U)
and
f^{-1}(U\vee X)
make then a covering of
X
by contractible open sets, and
cat
\,X=1
. Now the result follows from Corollary 2.
\square

9
The Sullivan completion,
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
, of a space
X

Throughout §9 we consider a connected space,
X
with a minimal Sullivan model

\varphi:(\land V,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{PL}(X).

Definition.
The adjoint map

|\varphi|:X\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\langle\land V\rangle,

is the
Sullivan completion
of
X
.

Proposition 9.1
.

(i)

\pi_{*}|\varphi|
converts Whitehead products in
\pi_{*}(X)
to constructions in
L_{X}
as set out in Proposition
8.2
.

(ii)

(
[
19
, Theorem 15.11]
)
. If
X
is a simply connected space and
H(X)
is a graded vector space of finite type then
\pi_{*}|\varphi|
factors as

\pi_{*}(X)\to\pi_{*}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}}).

The definition above depends on the choice for any space
X
of a minimal Sullivan model
\land V
. However, since two minimal Sullivan models
\land V,\land V^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{PL}(X)
are connected by a unique homotopy class of isomorphisms, it follows that for any minimal model
\land V^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{PL}(X)
, the homeomorphisms
\langle\land V^{\prime}\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\mathbb{Q}}
are connected by unique homotopy classes of isomorphisms. This identifies the vector spaces
V^{\prime}
, the groups
\pi_{*}\langle\land V^{\prime}\rangle
, and the enriched Lie algebras
L_{V^{\prime}}
, and we write

V_{X}=V^{\prime},\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})=\pi_{*}\langle\land V^{\prime}\rangle,\hskip 8.53581pt\mbox{and }L_{X}=L_{V^{\prime}}.

Definition.

L_{X}
is the homotopy Lie algebra of
X
.

In particular, (
16
) specializes to

\displaystyle\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})=V_{X}^{\vee}=sL_{X}.

(17)

Moreover, a continuous map
f:Y\to X
has a unique homotopy class of Sullivan representatives
\varphi:\land V_{Y}\leftarrow\land V_{X}
between the respective minimal Sullivan models. Now it follows from (
16
) that

\displaystyle\pi_{*}(f_{\mathbb{Q}})=\pi_{*}\langle\varphi\rangle=sL_{f}.

(18)

This
makes
\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
effectively computable in many cases, which in turn can provide information about
\pi_{*}(X)
. In particular,
Proposition
8.2
provides an explicit description of the Whitehead products in
\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
and of the group structure of
\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
in terms of the minimal model
(\land V,d)
of
X
.

However
H(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
remains mysterious, and it is unknown what conditions are necessary and or sufficient for
H^{n}(X_{\mathbb{Q}};\mathbb{Z})
,
n\geq 1
, to be a rational vector space. We do have

Proposition 9.2
.

Suppose
\varphi:(\land V,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}A_{PL}(X)
is a minimal Sullivan model of a space
X
. Then
(\land V,d)
is a retract of the minimal Sullivan model of
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and so
H(X)
is a retract of
H(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

proof.

Let
(\land W,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle
be the minimal Sullivan model. Since (
15
)
A_{PL}|\varphi|\circ\iota_{\land V}\sim\varphi
the homotopy commutative diagram

\textstyle{A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A_{PL}|\varphi|}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(X)}
\textstyle{\land V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\land V}}
\textstyle{\land W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\land V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}

exhibits
(\land V,d)
as a homotopy retract of
(\land W,d)
.
\square

Corollary.
Any continuous map
f:Y\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
extends to a map
Y_{\mathbb{Q}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}.

proof.
Compose
f_{\mathbb{Q}}
with the retraction
(X_{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
\square

Now observe that the retraction

\xi:\land^{\geq 1}V\to V

with kernel
\land^{\geq 2}V
satisfies
\xi\circ d=0
and so induces a map
H(\xi):H^{\geq 1}(\land V)\to V
.
On the other hand, denote by
hur:\pi_{*}(X)\to H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
the Hurewicz homomorphism, and recall that

\pi_{*}(|\varphi|):\pi_{*}(X)\to\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})=V^{\vee}.

Proposition 9.3
.

(
[
19
, Proposition 1.19]
) For
\alpha\in H^{\geq 1}(\land V)
and
\beta\in\pi_{*}(X)
,

<H(\varphi)\alpha,hur\,\beta>=<H(\xi)(\alpha),\pi_{*}(|\varphi|)\beta>;

Moreover, because of the naturality of the Hurewicz homomorphism, this follows from a commutative diagram valid for any minimal Sullivan algebra,
\land V
. Recall from §7 the natural morphism
\iota_{\land V}:\land V\to A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle
adjoint to
id_{\langle\land V\rangle}
. Proposition
8.2
specializes to (and follows from) the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{*}\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{hur}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{V^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\xi)^{\vee}}
\textstyle{H_{*}(\langle\land V\rangle;\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\iota_{\land V})^{\vee}}
\textstyle{H^{\geq 1}(\land V)^{\vee}}

in which the upper isomorphism is the isomorphism (
14
). Note that the generating space
V
can always be chosen so that
\xi:\mbox{ker}\,d\to V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
. In this case in the diagram above
H^{\geq 1}(\land V)^{\vee}
may be replaced by
(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee}
.

9.1
The Sullivan completion of a free group,
G

Suppose
b_{1},\dots,b_{s}
freely generate a free group
G
. The classifying space for
G
is then

X=S^{1}_{b_{1}}\vee\dots\vee S^{1}_{b_{s}},

and
b_{i}
is the homotopy class of the inclusion of
S^{1}_{b_{i}}
in
X
. In particular,
\pi_{*}(X)=\pi_{1}(X)=G
.

On the other hand, (Corollary 2 to Proposition
8.4
), the minimal Sullivan model of
X
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
8.4
. Therefore it is a quadratic Sullivan algebra
(\land V,d_{1})
and the inclusion

j:V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}{\longrightarrow}\land^{\geq 1}V

induces an isomorphism
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H^{1}(\land V)=H^{1}(X)
.
It follows that
V=V^{1}
.

Now recall that the quasi-isomorphism
\varphi:(\land V,d_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}A_{PL}(X)
induces via adjointness a continuous map

|\varphi|:X\to\langle\land V\rangle=X_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Definition.
The group morphism
\pi_{1}|\varphi|:G=\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
is the
Sullivan completion
of
G
.

We now recall three important constructions:

\bullet

The
homotopy Lie algebra
,
L_{V}
, of
(\land V,d_{1})
, a complete enriched Lie algebra, defined (§8) via the quadratic differential,
d_{1}
. By construction

sL_{V}=V^{\vee}

as graded vector spaces.

\bullet

The
group

G_{L_{V}}
(§1.2) connected with
L_{V}
by natural inverse bijections

\textstyle{L_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exp}
\textstyle{G_{L_{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\log}

\bullet

A
natural bijection of sets
(12)

\tau_{V}:\pi_{1}\langle\land V\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}V^{\vee}.

With
L_{V}
and
V^{\vee}
identified as above, (
[
19
, Theorem 2.4]
) provides a commutative diagram,

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}|\varphi|}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{L_{V}}
\textstyle{G_{L_{V}},\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\log}

in which
\sigma_{V}
is a natural isomorphism of groups. We denote by
\log_{G}
the composite

\log_{G}:G\longrightarrow G_{L_{V}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\log}}{{\longrightarrow}}L_{V}.

The constructions above combine to form the commutative diagram,

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{V}\circ\pi_{1}|\varphi|}
\textstyle{V^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{j^{\vee}}
\textstyle{sL_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(1)}
\textstyle{(2)}
\textstyle{G/G^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{sL_{V}/L_{V}^{2},}

which we now establish.

First, the isomorphism
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H^{1}(X)
dualizes to an isomorphism
H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}
. The composite

G\to V^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j^{\vee}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Q})

is a set map
G\to H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Q})
. It follows from Proposition
9.3
that this is
hur\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, where
hur
is the classical Hurewicz homomorphism. Thus it induces an isomorphism
G/G^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}
. This establishes
(1)
.

Second, the quasi-isomorphism
\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land V,d_{1})
admits a cdga retraction
\rho:(\land V,d_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})
, where
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}
is given the trivial product. Thus Proposition
8.4
identifies
L_{V}
as a profree Lie algebra
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T^{\prime}}
, in which
\rho^{\vee}:(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}T^{\prime}
. Since
j^{\vee}\circ\rho^{\vee}=id_{(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}}
it follows that ker
\,j^{\vee}
is a direct summand of
T^{\prime}
. But for
v\in V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}
, and for
x,y\in L_{V}
,

<v,j^{\vee}s[x,y]>=<d_{1}j(v),s[x,y]>=0.

Therefore
L_{V}^{(2)}\subset\mbox{ker}\,j^{\vee}
and so
L^{(2)}=\mbox{ker}\,j^{\vee}
. This establishes (2).

Next, let
\overline{J}
denote the augmentation ideal of
\overline{UL_{V}}
(§1.2) and recall that the right adjoint representation of
L_{V}
extends to a representation of
\overline{J}
.

Proposition 9.4
.

(i) The elements
\log_{G}b_{i}
are the basis of a subspace
T\subset L_{V}
for which
L_{V}=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
.

(ii) If
c,d\in G
then

\log_{G}cd\in\log_{G}c+\log_{G}d+\frac{1}{2}[\log_{G}c,\log_{G}d]+[\log_{G}c,\log_{G}d]\cdot\overline{J},

and

\log_{G}(cdc^{-1}d^{-1})\in[\log_{G}c,\log_{G}d]+[\log_{G}c,\log_{G}d]\cdot\overline{J}.

proof.
(i) follows from (18), diagrams (1) and (2) above, and the commutative diagram (
[
19
, Theorem 2.2]
),

\textstyle{G_{L_{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\log}
\textstyle{L_{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{L_{V}}/G_{L_{V}}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\log}
\textstyle{L_{V}/L_{V}^{(2)},}

and diagrams (1) and (2) above.

(ii) A naturality argument reduces this to the case
G
is freely generated by
c
and
d
. In this case
T=\mathbb{Q}\log_{G}c\oplus\mathbb{Q}\log_{G}d
,
[T,T=\mathbb{Q}[\log_{G}c,\log_{G}d]
and
L_{V}^{(3)}\subset[\log_{G}c,\log_{G}d]\cdot\overline{J}
. Thus (ii) follows immediately from (
[
19
, Proposition 2.4]
).

\square

9.2
Remarks

1. Let
\varphi:\land V\to A_{PL}(X)
and
\psi:\land W\to A_{PL}(Y)
be minimal Sullivan models. Then cf (
13
) we have bijections

[Y,X_{\mathbb{Q}}]\cong[\land V,A_{PL}(Y)]\cong[\land V,\land W].

2. By
[
17
, Proposition 17.13]
Sullivan completions induce a morphism

[\land V,\land W]\to[\langle\land W\rangle,\langle\land V\rangle].

This morphism is not always injective. For instance, if
Y
is an infinite wedge of spheres
S^{3}
, and
X=S^{3}
, then
\land V=(\land u,0)
with deg
\,u=3
, and
\land W\simeq(H(Y),0)
. Therefore

[\land V,\land W]\cong W^{3}\,\hskip 8.53581pt\mbox{and }[\langle\land W\rangle,\langle\land V\rangle]\cong H^{3}\langle\land W\rangle=(\pi_{3}\langle\land W\rangle)^{\vee}=\left((W^{3})^{\vee}\right)^{\vee}.

Suppose
\alpha\in H^{3}\langle\land W\rangle
is in the kernel of
H(|\psi|):H(\langle\land W\rangle)\to H(Y)
. This gives a map
g:Y_{\mathbb{Q}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
with
g\circ|\psi|\sim*\sim*\circ|\psi|
.

3. When
Y
is a simply connected CW complex of finite type, then (
[
17
, Theorem 17.12]
)
|\psi|:Y\to Y_{\mathbb{Q}}
is an isomorphism in rational cohomology and classical obstruction theory shows that in that case the morphism
[\land V,\land W]\to[\langle\land W\rangle,\langle\land V\rangle]
is a bijection.

4. Let
X=\langle\land V\rangle
be the spatial realization of the Sullivan quadratic model of the profree Lie algebra
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
where
T=T_{1}
is a countably infinite dimensional vector space. Then
H_{3}(X)\neq 0
and
H_{4}(X)\neq 0
.

We only provide the proof for
H_{3}(X)
. The proof for
H_{4}(X)
is similar.
Let
\omega\in\pi_{3}(X)
be a homotopy class corresponding by suspension to an element in
L_{2}
, but not in
[L_{1},L_{1}]
. Then
X
contains a simply connected finite CW complex
Y
such that
\omega
is in the image of
\pi_{3}(Y)
. We denote this element by
\omega_{Y}\in\pi_{3}(Y)
. It follows that
\omega_{Y}
is not decomposable, and by Lemma
9.1
below, the image
a_{Y}
of
\omega_{Y}
in
H_{3}(Y)
is non zero.

For any finite sub CW complex
Z
of
X
containing
Y
, the element
\omega_{Z}
induces also a non-zero element
a_{Z}
by the Hurewicz map, and by naturalness of the Hurewicz map, the map
H_{3}(Y)\to H_{3}(Z)
maps
a_{Y}
to
a_{Z}
. Now
X
is the union of the finite sub CW complexes
Z_{\alpha}
containing
Y
. It follows that
H_{3}(X)=\varinjlim_{\alpha}H_{3}(Z_{\alpha})
and therefore the family
a_{Z_{\alpha}}
induces a nonzero element in
H_{3}(X)
.

\square

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
L
be the homotopy Lie algebra of a finite type simply connected CW complex,
Y
.

(i)

If
L_{2}\neq[L_{1},L_{1}]
, then the Hurewicz map
\pi_{3}(Y)\to H_{3}(Y)
is non zero.

(ii)

If
L_{3}\neq[L_{1},L_{2}]
, then the Hurewicz map
\pi_{4}(Y)\to H_{4}(Y)
is non zero.

Proof.
Denote by
(\land V,d)
the Sullivan minimal model of
Y
. Then
V
is a finite type vector space. Therefore the brackets
[\,,\,]:\land^{2}L_{1}\to L_{2}
, and
L_{1}\otimes L_{2}\to L_{3}
are dual to the differentials
d:V^{3}\to\land^{2}V^{2}
, and
V^{4}\to V^{2}\otimes V^{3}
. Consider the situation (i); the proof for (ii) is similar. Since
L_{2}\neq[L_{1},L_{1}]
,
d
is not injective. This implies that
H^{3}(\land V)\to H^{3}(\land V/\land^{\geq 2}V)
is non zero. But this last map is the dual of the Hurewicz map. Thus if
\omega\in\pi_{3}(Y)\otimes\mathbb{Q}=(V^{3})^{\vee}
with
\omega(v)\neq 0
, then hur
(\omega)\neq 0
.

\square

9.3
\pi_{1}|\varphi|:\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})

Recall that for any group,
G
,
G^{n}
denotes the subgroup generated by iterated commutators of length
n
, and that for any graded Lie algebra,
L
,
L^{n}
is the span of the iterated commutators of length
n
. In particular a normal subgroup
Tor(G)
is defined by

Tor(G)=\{a\in G\,|\,\mbox{for each $n$ there is a $k$ such that }a^{k}\in G^{n}\}.

(Thus if
G
is abelian,
Tor(G)
is the subgroup of torsion elements.)

Proposition 9.5
.

If
H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is finitely generated then

\mbox{ker}(\pi_{1}|\varphi|:\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbb{Q}}))=Tor(\pi_{1}(X)).

proof.
For simplicity we denote
\pi_{1}(X)
by
G
. Then the classifying map
X\to BG
induces an isomorphism in
H_{1}(-;\mathbb{Z})
and, by a theorem (
[
8
, Theorem 5.2]
) of H. Hopf, a surjection in
H_{2}(-;\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore it induces an isomorphism in
H^{1}(-;\mathbb{Q})
and an injection in
H^{2}(-;\mathbb{Q})
. In particular the corresponding morphism of minimal Sullivan models restricts to an isomorphism

(\land V^{1},d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longleftarrow}}(\land W^{1},d).

This identifies
\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
with
\pi_{1}((BG)_{\mathbb{Q}})
and reduces the Proposition to the case
X=BG
.

Denote by
L
the component of degree
0
in the homotopy Lie algebra of
BG
, and by
G_{L}
the group
\pi_{1}((BG)_{\mathbb{Q}})
. Since (by hypothesis)
G/G^{2}
is finitely generated it follows that each
G^{n}/G^{n+1}
is finitely generated. In particular,

\mbox{ker}\,(G^{n-1}/G^{n}\to G^{n-1}/G^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})

is a torsion group. On the other hand (
[
19
, Theorem 7.5]
),
\log\circ\pi_{1}|\varphi|
induces isomorphisms

\psi_{n}:G^{n-1}/G^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}L^{n-1}/L^{n}.

Now suppose
a\in\mbox{ker}\,\pi_{1}|\varphi|
and that
a^{k}\in G^{n-1}
. Let
b=
image of
a^{k}
in
G^{n-1}/G^{n}
. Then
\psi_{n}b
is the image of
\log\pi_{1}|\varphi|a^{k}=k\log\pi_{1}|\varphi|a=0
. Thus
b
is in the kernel of
G^{n-1}/G^{n}\to G^{n-1}/G^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, and so

a^{\ell k}=b^{\ell}\in G^{n}.

It follows by induction that
a\in Tor(G)
.

In the reverse direction the same argument shows that if
a\in Tor(G)
, then for each
n
and some
k
,

k\log\pi_{1}|\varphi|a=\log\pi_{1}|\varphi|a^{k}\in L^{n}.

It follows that
\log\pi_{1}|\varphi|a\in\cap_{n}L^{n}=0
, and thus
a\in\mbox{ker}\pi_{1}|\varphi|
.
\square

9.4
The radical and center of
\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})

As recalled in §6, the category, cat
\,(\land V)
, is the least
m
(or
\infty
) for which
\land V
is a homotopy retract of
\land V/\land^{>m}V
. Moreover (
[
19
, Theorem 9.2]
),

\mbox{cat}\,(\land V)\leq\mbox{cat}\,X,

where cat
\,X
is the classical Lusternik-Schnirelmann category of
X
. On the other hand, the
radical
, rad
\,L_{V}
, of
L_{V}
is the sum of the solvable ideals of
L_{V}
. Thus combining (
[
12
, Theorem 1]
) with (
[
13
, Theorem C]
) yields

Proposition 9.6
.

Suppose cat
\,(X)<\infty
. Then

(i)

The sum of the solvable ideals, rad
\,L_{V}
, is finite dimensional and

\mbox{dim(rad}\,(L_{V})_{even})\leq\mbox{ cat}\,X.

(ii)

The union of the normal solvable subgroups of
\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
is a normal solvable subgroup of solv length
\leq
cat
\,(X)
.

proof.
(i) is immediate from the remarks above. For the proof of (ii) note that rad
(L_{V})_{0}
is a closed ideal since it is finite dimensional. Thus
\exp
(rad
(L_{V})_{0}
) is a normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
whose quotient is
\exp
(
(L_{V})_{0}/\mbox{rad}(L_{V})_{0})
. Therefore, since the center of
(L_{V})_{0}/\mbox{rad}(L_{V})_{0}
is zero,
\exp
(
L_{V}/\mbox{rad}(L_{V})_{0})
has no center and therefore no normal solvable subgroup.
\square

Now define
C_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})\subset\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
to be the subgroup of elements
\alpha
satisfying

\mbox{Wh}(\alpha,\beta)=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\beta\in\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}}).

It follows from Proposition
8.2
that
C_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
corresponds under
\exp
to the centre of the homotopy Lie algebra
L_{V}
. Thus Proposition
9.6
has the

Corollary.
If cat
\,X<\infty
then the group
C_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
is a finite dimensional rational vector space.

9.5
The Gottlieb groups
G_{*}(X)
and
G_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})

The
n^{th}
Gottlieb group
,
G_{n}(X)
, is the subgroup of
\pi_{n}(X)
formed by the homotopy classes
[f]
of maps
f:S^{n}\to X
such that
f\vee\mbox{id}:S^{n}\vee X\to X
extends to the product
S^{n}\times X
. The Whitehead products of
\alpha\in G_{*}(X)
and
\beta\in\pi_{*}(X)
are zero. In particular,
G_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})\subset C_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

More generally, if
g:Y\to X
is a continuous map, then the
n^{th}
Gottlieb group of
g
,
G_{n}(g)
is the subgroup of
\pi_{n}(X)
formed by the homotopy classes of maps
f:S^{n}\to X
such that the map
f\vee g:S^{n}\vee Y\to X
extends to
S^{n}\times Y
.

Finally, recall from (
13
) that for any minimal Sullivan algebra
\land W
,

\pi_{n}\langle\land W\rangle=[\land W,\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}a_{n}]

where
a_{n}
is the fundamental cohomology class in
H^{n}(S^{n})
. We define the
Gottlieb group
,

G_{n}(\land W)\subset\pi_{n}\langle\land W\rangle

to be the subgroup formed by those elements represented by morphisms
\sigma:\land W\to\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}a_{n}
such that
\sigma\times id:\land W\to(\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}a_{n})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land W
factors as

\land W\to(\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}a_{n})\otimes\land W\to(\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}a_{n})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land W.

Note here that

(\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}a_{n})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land W=\mathbb{Q}a_{n}\oplus\land W

and that
\sigma
is just the map

W=\land^{\geq 1}W/\land^{\geq 2}W\to\mathbb{Q}a_{n}.

Proposition 9.7
.

(i)

\pi_{*}|\varphi|:G_{*}(X)\to G_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

(ii)

G_{*}(\land V)=G_{*}(|\varphi|)=G_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

(iii)

G_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})\subset C_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

proof.
(i) If
f:S^{n}\to X
represents an element of
G_{*}(X)
we may extend
f\vee id_{X}

to
g:S^{n}\times X\to X
. Now Proposition
8.3
(iii) identifies
(S^{n}\times X)_{\mathbb{Q}}=S^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times X_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Thus we obtain
g_{\mathbb{Q}}:S^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times X_{\mathbb{Q}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
. It follows from (
16
) that
g_{\mathbb{Q}}
restricts to
|\varphi|\circ f:S^{n}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Thus
g_{\mathbb{Q}}:S^{n}\times X_{\mathbb{Q}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
extends
|\varphi|\circ f\vee id_{X_{\mathbb{Q}}}
.

(ii) We proceed in two steps.

Step One.

G_{*}(\land V)=G_{*}\langle\land V\rangle
.

Let
\land Z
denote the minimal model of
S^{n}
; Then (
13
)

\pi_{n}\langle\land V\rangle=\mbox{Hom}(V^{n},\mathbb{Q})=[\land V,\land Z]=[\land V,A_{PL}(S^{n})].

Thus an element in
G_{n}(\land V)
is represented by a morphism
\sigma:\land V\to\land Z
for which
\sigma\times_{\mathbb{Q}}id:\land V\to\land Z\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land V
factors up to homotopy as

\textstyle{\land V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\times_{\mathbb{Q}}id}
\textstyle{\land Z\otimes\land V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\land Z\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land V.}

The identifications in Proposition
8.2
then convert this diagram into the homotopy commutative diagram

\textstyle{\langle\land V\rangle}
\textstyle{\langle\land Z\rangle\times\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{n}\times\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\langle\land Z\rangle\vee\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\sigma\times_{\mathbb{Q}}id\rangle}
\textstyle{S^{n}\vee\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

in which the vertical arrows are the standard inclusions. In particular, the map

S^{n}\to\langle\land Z\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\langle\sigma\rangle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\langle\land V\rangle

represents
[\sigma]\in\pi_{n}(\land V)
.

In the reverse direction, suppose
\tau\in G_{n}\langle\land V\rangle
. Then we have the homotopy commutative diagram

\textstyle{\langle\land V\rangle}
\textstyle{S^{n}\times\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{n}\vee\langle\land V\rangle.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\tau,id)}

Let
\land W
be the minimal Sullivan model of
\langle\land V\rangle
. Then the identifications of Proposition
8.2
, together with the proof of Proposition
9.2
, convert this to the homotopy commutative diagram

\textstyle{\land V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(S^{n})\otimes A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\land Z\otimes\land W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\land Z\otimes\land V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(S^{n})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle}
\textstyle{\land Z\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\land Z\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land V.}

Step Two.
G_{n}(|\varphi|)=G_{n}(X_{\mathbb{Q}}
)
.

The injection
G_{n}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})\subset G_{n}(\varphi_{X})
is a direct consequence of the following commutative diagram associated to an element
[f]\in G_{n}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
,

\textstyle{S^{n}\vee X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\vee|\varphi|}
\textstyle{S^{n}\vee X_{\mathbb{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\vee id}
\textstyle{X_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\textstyle{S^{n}\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\times|\varphi|}
\textstyle{S^{n}\times X_{\mathbb{Q}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

For the reverse inclusion
G_{n}(|\varphi|)\subset G_{n}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
suppose
f:S^{n}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
represents an element in
G_{n}(|\varphi|)
. Then
|\varphi|\vee f
factors as

X\vee S^{n}\to X\times S^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}X_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Restricting
g_{\mathbb{Q}}
to
X_{\mathbb{Q}}\times S^{n}
, and denoting by
r:(X_{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
the retraction (Proposition
8.1
(iii)), then gives the commutative diagram,

\textstyle{X\vee S^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\times S^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\textstyle{X_{\mathbb{Q}}\vee S^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\mathbb{Q}}\times S^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\mathbb{Q}}}
\textstyle{(X_{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{Q}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}

This gives
[f]\in G_{n}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

(iii) This is immediate from the definitions and Proposition
9.6
(ii).

\square

Recall next (
[
19
, §3.3]
) that a
minimal Sullivan extension
is a morphism,

\land W\to\land W\otimes\land V,\hskip 28.45274ptw\mapsto w\otimes 1,

of Sullivan algebras in which
\land W
and the quotient
(\land V,d_{V})=\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\land W}(\land W\otimes\land V)
are minimal Sullivan algebras. However the differential
d
in
\land W\otimes\land V
may have a component
d_{0}
of wedge degree
0
:
d_{0}:W\leftarrow V
. The dual of
d_{0}
is then a linear map

\delta:W^{\vee}\to V^{\vee}

of degree
-1
. Recall also that
V^{\vee}=\mbox{Hom}(V,\mathbb{Q})\supset G_{*}(\land V)
.

Proposition 9.8
.

With the notation and hypotheses above
\delta:W^{\vee}\to V^{\vee}
is injective, and

\mbox{Im}\,\delta\subset G_{*}(\land V).

In particular Im
\,\delta
suspends to a subspace in the center of
L_{V}
.

proof.
Fix
n
and choose a cdga retraction

\xi:(\land W,d)\to(\mathbb{Q}\oplus d_{0}V^{n},0)

with
\land^{\geq 2}W\subset\mbox{ker}\,\xi
. Then extend
\mathbb{Q}\oplus d_{0}V^{n}
to an acyclic cdga

\land U\otimes(\mathbb{Q}\oplus d_{0}V^{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}

with
U=U^{\geq n}
, and

d:U^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}d_{0}V^{n}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }d:U^{>n}\to\land^{\geq 1}U\otimes d_{0}V^{n}.

Now applying
-\otimes_{\land W}(\land W\oplus d_{0}V)
defines a surjective quasi-isomorphism

\rho:\land U\otimes(\mathbb{Q}\oplus d_{0}V^{n})\otimes\land V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land V,d_{V}).

Thus
\rho
has a left inverse
\sigma:(\land V,d_{V})\to\land U\otimes(\mathbb{Q}\oplus d_{0}V^{n})\otimes\land V
, and it is immediate from the definitions that
\sigma
may be chosen so that
\sigma v=1\otimes v
,
v\in V^{<n}
and

\sigma v=1\otimes v-\alpha(d_{0}v),

where
\alpha
is inverse to the isomorphism
U^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}d_{0}V^{n}
. Division by
d_{0}V^{n}
and by
U^{>n}
then yields the sequence of morphisms

(\land V,d_{V})\to(\land U^{n},0)\otimes(\land V,d_{V})\to(\land U^{n},0)\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land V,d_{V}).

In particular, any morphism
\land U^{n}\to H(S^{n})
induces a sequence

(\land V,d_{V})\to H(S^{n})\otimes(\land V,d_{V})\to H(S^{n})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land V,d_{V}).

In particular, the resulting morphism
(\land V,d_{V})\to H(S^{n})
is an element of
G_{n}(\land V)
.

Finally, this element of
G_{n}(\land V)
is determined by the composite
V^{n}\to U^{n}\to H^{n}(S^{n})
, and by construction the map
V^{n}\to U^{n}
is given by
v\mapsto-\alpha(d_{0}v)
. This gives the commutative triangles

\textstyle{U^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{d_{0}V^{n}}
\textstyle{V^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}}

and
\textstyle{(U_{n})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(d_{0}V^{n})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{(V^{n})^{\vee},}

This identifies the elements in Im
\,\delta
as elements in
G_{n}(\land V)
and, since
V^{n}\to d_{0}V^{n}
is surjective,
\delta
is injective.

\square

Proposition 9.9
.

If
\land V
is a minimal Sullivan algebra and cat
(\land V)<\infty
, then

(i)

G_{2n}(\land V)=0
,
n\geq 1
, and

(ii)

dim
G_{*}(\land V)\leq
cat
(\land V)
.

proof.
(i) Let
B=\land a/(a^{2})
, with deg
\,a=2n
. We suppose that there exists a morphism
\varphi:\land V\to B
that is a representative of an element of
G_{2n}(\land V)
. If
\varphi\neq 0
there is an element
z_{0}\in V^{2n}
with
\varphi(z_{0})=a
.

Now let
(\land W,d):=\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\land V^{<2n}}(\land V,d)
. By (
[
19
, Proposition 9.3]
) cat
\,(\land W)\leq
cat
(\land V)<\infty
. We denote by
\overline{\varphi}:\land W\to B
the map induced by
\varphi
. Since
\overline{\varphi}
is also in
G_{n}(\land W)
, we get a morphism

\psi:\land W\to\land W\otimes B

with
\psi(w)-w\in\land W\otimes B^{+}
and
\psi(z_{0})=z_{0}+a.

By iteration this gives

\psi(q):\land W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\land W\otimes B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi\otimes id}}{{\longrightarrow}}\land W\otimes B\otimes B\to\dots\to\land W\otimes B^{\otimes^{q}}.

In particular,
\psi(q)(z_{0}^{q})-a^{\otimes^{q}}\in\land^{+}W\otimes B^{\otimes^{q}}
. By construction
d(z_{0})=0
. Since cat
\land W<\infty
, there is
y\in\land W
with
dy=z_{0}^{q}
. It follows that
\psi(q)(z_{0}^{q})
is a boundary which is impossible. This shows that
\varphi=0
.

(ii). Decompose
\land V
as a Sullivan extension
\land V=\land U^{\leq n}\otimes\land Z^{\geq n}
in which
G_{n}(\land V)\to G_{n}(\land Z)
is injective and
<v,x>=1
for some
x\in G_{n}(\land Z)
and some cycle
v\in Z^{n}
. Since (
[
19
, Proposition 9.3]
) cat
(\land Z)\leq
cat
(\land V)
, it is sufficient to show that dim
\,G_{*}(\land Z)\leq
cat
(\land Z)
. In other words, without loss of generality we may suppose
V=V^{\geq n}
and that
x\in G_{n}(\land V)
and
v
is a cycle in
V^{n}
. Since (i) implies that
n
is odd we also have

H(S^{n})=\land\widehat{v}\cong\land v.

Moreover, since
x\in G_{n}(\land V)
, by definition we obtain the sequence of morphisms

\land V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\land\widehat{v}\otimes\land V\longrightarrow\land\widehat{v}\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land V

induced by
x
.

Now set
W=\{w\in V\,|,<w,x>=0\}
. Then
W=\cup_{k\geq 1}W(k)
defined by
W(1)=W\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
and
W(k+1)=W\cap d^{-1}(\land v\otimes\land W(k))
. Suppose next, by induction on
k
that
\land W(k)
is preserved by
d
and that

\varphi w-w\in\widehat{v}\land v\otimes\land W(k),\hskip 28.45274ptw\in W(k).

iC1-.
