Finally, fix a basis element
z
in a direct summand of
W(k)
in
W(k+1)
. Then by definition,

dz=v\Psi_{1}+\Psi_{2}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }\varphi z=\widehat{v}\land(v\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2})+z,

where
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}\in\land W(k)
. This yields

d\varphi z=\widehat{v}\,v\,d\Phi_{1}-\widehat{v}\,d\Phi_{2}+dz

\varphi dz=(\widehat{v}+v)\varphi\Psi_{1}+\varphi\Psi_{2}=v\,\Psi_{1}+\Psi_{2}+\widehat{v}\,\Psi_{1}+\widehat{v}\,v\,\Gamma,

with
\Gamma\in\land W(k)
. Since
\varphi dz=d\varphi z
and
dz=v\Psi_{1}+\Psi_{2}
, it follows that

\widehat{v}v\,d\Phi_{1}-\widehat{v}\,d\Phi_{2}=\widehat{v}\,\Psi_{1}+\widehat{v}v\Gamma.

In other words,
\Psi_{1}=-d\Phi_{2}
.

Now extend the splitting to
W(k+1)
by replacing
z
by
z-v\Phi_{2}
. It is straightforward to check that our conditions are satisfied. Thus by induction we may write
\land V=\land v\otimes\land W
with
\land W
preserved by
d
. It follows that
G_{*}(\land V)=\mathbb{Q}x\oplus G_{*}(\land W)
. Since by (
[
19
, Corollary 9.3]
), cat
(\land W)=
cat
(\land V)-1
, (ii) follows by induction on cat
(\land V)
.
\square

10
Wedges of topological spaces

10.1
The minimal Sullivan model of
X\vee Y

If
X
and
Y
are connected spaces then the surjections
H(X\vee Y)\to H(X),H(Y)
define an isomorphism
H(X\vee Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H(X)\times_{\mathbb{Q}}H(Y)
. It follows that Sullivan models
\land W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}A_{PL}(X)
and
\land Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}A_{PL}(Y)
extend to a quasi-isomorphism

\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{PL}(X\vee Y).

This identifies a Sullivan model
\land T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
as a Sullivan model for
X\vee Y
.

On the other hand, for any two Sullivan algebras
\land W
and
\land Q
the surjection
\land W\otimes\land Q\to\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
extends to a quasi-isomorphism

\varphi:\land T:=\land W\otimes\land Q\otimes\land R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q

from a minimal Sullivan extension. Since
\land W\otimes\land Q\to\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
is surjective we may choose
R\subset T
so that
\varphi(R)=0
. It follows that
\land T
is a Sullivan algebra, minimal if
\land W
and
\land Z
are minimal, and that in any case the quotient
(\land R,\overline{d})
is a minimal Sullivan algebra.

Proposition 10.1
.

With the notation and hypotheses above,

(i)

With appropriate choice of generating space
R\subset\land R
,

R\cap\mbox{ker}\,\overline{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{\geq 1}(\land R,\overline{d}).

In particular (Proposition
8.4
) the homotopy Lie algebra
L_{R}
is profree.

(ii)

If
\land W
and
\land Q
are quadratic Sullivan algebras then
\land T
is quadratic.

(iii)

In general, the quadratic Sullivan algebra
(\land T,d_{1})
for
\land T
is the Sullivan model of
(\land W,d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{1})
.

Remark.
This result is analogous to the fact that the usual fibre of the injection
X\vee Y\to X\times Y
is the join of
\Omega X
and
\Omega Y
and thus a suspension. (But note that
(X\vee Y)_{\mathbb{Q}}
may be different from
X_{\mathbb{Q}}\vee Y_{\mathbb{Q}}
.)

proof.

(i) Let
\land W\otimes\land U_{W}
and
\land Q\otimes\land U_{Q}
denote the respective acyclic closures.
These, together with
\varphi:\land T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\land}}W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
, yield the quasi-isomorphisms

\land R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longleftarrow}}\land T\otimes_{\land W\otimes\land Q}(\land W\otimes\land U_{W}\otimes\land Q\otimes\land U_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}A:=(\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q)\otimes\land U_{W}\otimes\land U_{Q}.

Now divide
A
by the ideal generated by
W
to obtain the short exact sequence

0\to\land^{\geq 1}W\otimes\land U_{W}\otimes\land U_{Q}\to A\to\land Q\otimes\land U_{W}\otimes\land U_{Q}\to 0.

Next, decompose the differential in
\land W\otimes\land U_{W}
in the form
d=d_{1}+d^{\prime}
with
d_{1}(W)\subset\land^{2}W
,
d_{1}(U_{W})\subset W\otimes\land U_{W}
,
d^{\prime}(W)\subset\land^{\geq 3}W
and
d^{\prime}(U_{W})\subset\land^{\geq 2}W\otimes\land U_{W}
. Then
d_{1}
is a differential and
(\land W\otimes\land U_{W},d_{1})
is the acyclic closure of
(\land W,d_{1})
. Choose a direct summand,
S
, of
d_{1}(\land^{\geq 1}U_{W})
in
W\otimes\land U_{W}
. Then
I=(\land^{\geq 2}W\otimes\land U_{W})\oplus S
is acyclic for the differential
d_{1}
and therefore also for the differential
d
.
Thus
J=I\otimes\land U_{Q}
is an acyclic ideal in
A
and
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}A/J
.

On the other hand, consider the short exact sequence

0\to d(\land U_{W})\otimes\land U_{Q}\to A/J\to\land Q\otimes\land U_{W}\otimes\land U_{Q}\to 0

in which
d(\land U_{W})\otimes\land U_{Q}
is an ideal with trivial multiplication and trivial differential. It follows from the long homology sequence that

\mathbb{Q}\oplus d(\land U_{W})\otimes\land^{\geq 1}U_{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}A/J.

Since
\land R\simeq A\simeq A/J
, this completes the proof of (i).

(ii) Assign
\land W
and
\land Q
wedge degree as a second degree and assign
U_{W}
and
U_{Q}
second degree
0
. Then
(\land W\otimes\land U_{W},d_{1})
and
(\land Q\otimes\land U_{Q},d_{1})
are the respective acyclic closures of
(\land W,d_{1})
and
(\land Q,d_{1})
, and
d_{1}
increases the second degree by 1. Now
{\varphi}
and
{T}

may be constructed so that
{R}
is equipped with a second gradation for which
d
increases the second degree by one and
{\varphi}
is bihomogeneous of degree zero.

The argument in the proof of (i) now yields a sequence of bihomogeneous quasi-isomorphisms connecting

\mathbb{Q}\oplus\left(d_{1}(\land^{+}U_{W})\otimes\land^{+}U_{Q}\right)\simeq\land{R}.

Thus
H^{\geq 1}(\land{R})
is concentrated in second degree 1. Denote by
S\subset R
the subspace of elements of second degree 1. Then
(\land S,d)
is a quadratic Sullivan algebra. Moreover by (i), any cycle
\Phi\in\land^{2}S
is a boundary, necessarily of an element
v\in S
of wedge degree 1. Thus
H(\land S)
has no homology of wedge degree 2. Now Lemma
6.2
implies that
S\cap\mbox{ker}\,d=H^{\geq 1}(\land S)
. But if
S\neq R
then for some
z\in R
,
dz\in\land S
. This implies that for some
\Psi\in\land S
,
z-d\Psi
is a cycle and therefore is in
S
. This gives
S=R
. Thus
\land T
is quadratic and
\varphi
preserves wedge degrees.

(iii) Denote by
d=\sum_{i\geq 1}d_{i}
the differentials in
\land W
and
\land Q
, where
d_{i}
increases the wedge degree by
i
. Then (ii) constructs a quasi-isomorphism

\varphi:(\land T,d_{1})=(\land W\otimes\land Q\otimes\land R,d_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land W,d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{1})

in which
\varphi
is surjective and preserves wedge degree. In particular,

H(\mbox{Ker}\,\varphi,d_{1})=0.

Now we deform
d_{1}
to a differential
d=\sum_{i\geq 1}d_{i}
in
\land T
with the following properties

\bullet

d
restricts to the original differentials in
\land W
and
\land Q
.

\bullet

d_{i}:R\to\mbox{Ker}\,\varphi
,
i\geq 2
.

Write
R=\cup_{\neq\geq 0}R_{n}
, with
R_{0}=R\cap d_{1}^{-1}(\land W\otimes\land Q)
and for
n>0
,

R_{n}=R\cap d_{1}^{-1}(\land W\otimes\land Q\otimes\land R_{n-1}).

We define the derivations
d_{2},\dots,d_{q},...
on
R
by induction on
n
with the property that

d_{i}(R)\subset(\land^{+}R\otimes\land(W\otimes\land Q))\,\cap\,\land^{i+1}(R\oplus W\oplus Q)

and
(d_{1}+\sum_{j\geq 2}d_{j})^{2}=0
on
R_{n}
.

So suppose this is done on
R_{n-1}
and
z\in R_{n}
. We construct the
d_{p}
by induction on
p
. So suppose
d_{2},\dots,d_{p-1}
constructed such that for
2\leq q\leq p-1
, we have

(d_{1}d_{q}+d_{2}d_{q-1}+\dots+d_{q}d_{1})z=0.

Then let
R(z)=(d_{p}d_{1}+d_{p-1}d_{2}+\dots+d_{2}d_{p-1})(z)
. Since
d_{1}R(z)=0
and
R(z)\in\mbox{Ker}\,\varphi
, there is an element
u\in\mbox{Ker}\,\varphi
with
R(z)=-d_{1}(u)
. We define
d_{p}(x)=u
. It follows by induction on
p
that
d_{p}
increases wedge degree by
p
. Thus
d_{p}z=0
if
p>n
. We set
d=\sum_{i\geq 1}d_{i}
.

It is immediate from the construction that
d^{2}=0
and that the two required properties are satisfied. In particular
(\land T,d)
is a Sullivan algebra and since
\varphi
is a quasi-isomorphism with respect to
d_{1}
,

\varphi:(\land T,d)\to(\land W,d)\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d)

is a Sullivan model. Finally, the associated quadratic Sullivan algebra is by (ii) the Sullivan model of
(\land W,d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{1})
.

\square

Example 1.
If
\land W
and
\land Q
are minimal Sullivan algebras then (Proposition
10.1
) the surjection
\land W\otimes\land Q\to\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
extends to a quasi-isomorphism

\land T=\land W\otimes\land Q\otimes\land R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q

in which
\land T
is a minimal Sullivan algebra and the homotopy Lie algebra,
L_{R}
, is profree. Thus
\langle\land R\rangle
is rationally wedge-like.

2.
If one of the spaces
X
and
Y
has rational homology of finite type then the homotopy fibre of
(X\vee Y)_{\mathbb{Q}}\to(X\times Y)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is rationally wedge-like.

In fact, in this case if
\land W
and
\land Q
are respectively the minimal Sullivan models of
X
and
Y
then the inclusion
\land W\otimes\land Q\to\land T
is a Sullivan representative for the inclusion
i:X\vee Y\to X\times Y
. This identifies
i_{\mathbb{Q}}
as the map
\langle\land W\otimes\land Q\rangle\leftarrow\langle\land T\rangle
and it follows from (
[
17
, Proposition 17.9]
) that its homotopy fibre is
\langle\land R\rangle
.

10.2
Free products and the homotopy Lie algebra of
X\vee Y

The category of enriched Lie algebras has free products. In fact the classical construction of the free product,
L\amalg L^{\prime}
, of two graded Lie algebras extends naturally to enriched Lie algebras,
(L,\{I_{\alpha}\})
and
(L^{\prime},\{I^{\prime}_{\beta}\})
, with the enriched structure given by the surjections

\xi_{\alpha,\beta,n}:L\,\amalg\,L^{\prime}\to L_{\alpha}\,\amalg\,L^{\prime}_{\beta}\,/\,(L_{\alpha}\,\amalg\,L^{\prime}_{\beta})^{n}.

Its completion will be denoted by
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}
, and almost by definition,

L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{L}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{L^{\prime}}.

Definition.

L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}
is the
free product
of
L
and
L^{\prime}
.

Remarks. 1.
As in the classical case
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}
is equipped with inclusions
\textstyle{L,L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}}
, and here if
L
and
L^{\prime}
are complete the images are
closed sub Lie algebras. Moreover these inclusions extend to a
linear
inclusion

\textstyle{L\oplus L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}}

into a closed subspace of the free product.

2.
If
C\subset L
and
C^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime}
are closed subspaces, we denote by

C\oplus C^{\prime}\subset C\,\widehat{\amalg}\,C^{\prime}\subset L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}

the closure of the subspace of iterated Lie brackets of sequences of elements alternating between
C
and
C^{\prime}
.

Lemma 10.1
.

(i). With the notation above, any two morphisms
f:(L,\{I_{\alpha}\})\to E
and
g:(L^{\prime},\{I_{\beta}^{\prime}\})\to E
into a complete enriched Lie algebra extend uniquely to a morphism

L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}\to E.

This identifies
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}
as a coproduct in the category of complete Lie algebras, and characterizes
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}
up to natural isomorphism.

(ii). Suppose a profree Lie algebra
L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
, and
T=T(1)\oplus T(2)
with
T(1)
and
T(2)
closed subspaces. Then
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(1)}
and
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{(T(2)}
are the closures of the Lie algebras generated by
T(1)
and
T(2)
, and

\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(1)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(2)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}.

proof.
(i) Let
\{J_{\gamma}\}
denote the enriched structure for
E
. Then for each
\gamma
there are indices
\alpha(\gamma),\beta(\gamma)
such that
f
and
g
factor to yield morphisms

f_{\gamma}:L_{\alpha(\gamma)}\to E_{\gamma}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }g_{\gamma}:L^{\prime}_{\beta(\gamma)}\to E_{\gamma}.

Moreover, since
E_{\gamma}^{n(\gamma)}=0
, some
n(\gamma)
,
f_{\gamma}\amalg g_{\gamma}
factors to yield a morphism

h_{\gamma}:(L_{\alpha(\gamma)}\amalg L^{\prime}_{\beta(\gamma)})/(L_{\alpha(\gamma)}\amalg L^{\prime}_{\beta(\gamma)})^{n}\longrightarrow E_{\gamma}.

Since
E
is complete these extend to

h:=\varprojlim_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}:L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}\to E.

The uniqueness is immediate.

(ii) The inclusion
T(1)
,
T(2)\to T
extend to a morphism
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(1)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(2)}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
, with inverse given by the extension to
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
of
T=T(1)\oplus T(2)\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(1)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(2)}
.
\square

Proposition 10.2
.

Denote by
L_{X}
and
L_{Y}
the homotopy Lie algebras of connected spaces
X
and
Y
.
Then the inclusions
X,Y\to X\vee Y
induce an isomorphism

L_{X\vee Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}L_{X}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L_{Y}.

In particular, the correspondence
X\mapsto L_{X}
preserves coproducts.

Proposition
10.2
is an immediate consequence of the explicit description in Proposition
10.3
below of the homotopy Lie algebra of a fibre product
\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
of any two minimal Sullivan algebras.
Recall from Proposition
10.1
that the natural surjection
\land W\otimes\land Q\to\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
extends to a minimal Sullivan model

\varphi:\land T:=\land W\otimes\land Q\otimes\land R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q.

Denote the corresponding homotopy Lie algebras (§8) by
L_{W},L_{Q},L_{T}
and
L_{R}
. In particular, this identifies
L_{T}
as the homotopy Lie algebra of a minimal Sullivan model of
\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
.
The inclusions
\land W,\land Q\to\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land Q
then lift to unique based homotopy classes of morphisms
\land W,\land Q\to\land T
, and thus induce morphisms
L_{T}\to L_{W},L_{Q}
. These provide a surjection
L_{T}\to L_{W}\times L_{Q}
.

Proposition 10.3
.

With the hypotheses and notation above,

(i)

\varphi
induces a quasi-isomorphism
(\land T,d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{1})
of associated quadratic Sullivan algebras.

(ii)

The surjection
L_{T}\to L_{W}\times L_{Q}
factors as

\textstyle{L_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{L_{W}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L_{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{W}\times L_{Q},}

and
\sigma
is natural with respect to morphisms
\land W\to\land W^{\prime}
and
\land Q\to\land Q^{\prime}
.

(iii)

If
f:L\to L^{\prime}
and
g:E\to E^{\prime}
are morphisms of complete enriched Lie algebras, then

f\,\widehat{\amalg}\,g:L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,E\to L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,E^{\prime}

is surjective if and only if
f
and
g
are surjective.

Corollary.
If
L
and
L^{\prime}
are complete enriched Lie algebras then the kernel of

L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}\to L\times L^{\prime}

is profree. Moreover, if
L
and
L^{\prime}
are both profree then
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}
is also profree.

proof of the Corollary.
Apply Proposition
10.1
to identify the kernel as a profree Lie algebra
L_{R}
. If now
L
and
L^{\prime}
are profree then by Proposition
8.4
there are quasi-isomorphisms

\land W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}\oplus S\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }\land W^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Q}\oplus S^{\prime}

with
S\cdot S=0=S^{\prime}\cdot S^{\prime}
, and vanishing differentials in
S
and
S^{\prime}
. Thus
\land W\times_{\mathbb{Q}}\land W^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{Q}\oplus S\oplus S^{\prime}
, and its homotopy Lie algebra,
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}
, is profree by Proposition
8.4
.

\square

proof of Proposition
10.3
.
(i) As recalled at the start of §8,
L_{T},L_{W}
and
L_{Q}
depend only on the quadratic Sullivan algebras
(\land T,d_{1})
,
(\land W,d_{1})
and
(\land Q,d_{1})
. Moreover, by Proposition
10.1
,

(\land T,d_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land W,d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{1}).

Therefore for any quadratic Sullivan algebra
(\land V,d_{1})
, the based homotopy classes of morphisms satisfy

\begin{array}[]{ll}[(\land V,d_{1}),(\land T,d_{1})]_{*}&=[(\land V,d_{1}),(\land W,d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{1})]*\\
&=[(\land V,d_{1}),(\land W,d_{1})]_{*}\times[(\land V,d_{1}),(\land Q,d_{1})]*.\end{array}

(ii) Proposition
4.1
translates this equality to give

{\mathcal{C}}(L_{T},L_{V})={\mathcal{C}}(L_{W},L_{V})\times{\mathcal{C}}(L_{Q},L_{V}),

where
{\mathcal{C}}(-,-)
denotes the set of morphisms in the category of enriched Lie algebras. This identifies
L_{T}=L_{W}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L_{Q}
.
The naturality is a standard diagram chasing.

(iii) To show that if
f
and
g
are surjective then
f\,\widehat{\amalg}\,g
is surjective, it is sufficient to consider the case
g=id_{E}:E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle=}}{{\longrightarrow}}E
. In this case we have the row-exact commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\,\widehat{\amalg}\,id_{E}}
\textstyle{L\times E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\times id_{E}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\times E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

and so it is also sufficient to show that
h
is surjective.

Now let
\land W
,
\land W^{\prime}
and
\land Q
be, respectively the quadratic models of
L
,
L^{\prime}
and
E
, and let
\varphi:\land W^{\prime}\to\land W
be the morphism corresponding to
f
. Then the restriction
\varphi:W^{\prime}\to W
dualizes to
f
, and so
\varphi
is injective. In particular,
\varphi
extends to an inclusion

\varphi:\land W^{\prime}\otimes\land U_{W^{\prime}}\to\land W\otimes\land U_{W}

of the acyclic closures, restricting to an inclusion
U_{W^{\prime}}\to U_{W}
.

On the other hand, the diagram above is the diagram of homotopy Lie algebras associated with a commutative diagram,

\textstyle{\land W\otimes\land Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\land W\otimes\land Q\otimes\land R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\land R}
\textstyle{\land W^{\prime}\otimes\land Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi\otimes id}
\textstyle{\land W^{\prime}\otimes\land Q\otimes\land R^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\land R^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}

which identifies
L_{\sigma}
with
h
.

Moreover, the computations in the proof of Proposition
10.1
identify

H(\sigma)=\varphi:\mathbb{Q}\oplus d(\land U_{W^{\prime}})\to\mathbb{Q}\oplus d(\land U_{W}).

It follows that
H(\sigma)
is injective. But since
L_{R}
and
L_{R^{\prime}}
are profree, Lemma
4.1
identifies

L_{R}/L_{R}^{(2)}=H^{\geq 1}(\land R)^{\vee}\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }L_{R^{\prime}}/L_{R^{\prime}}^{(2)}=H^{\geq 1}(\land R^{\prime})^{\vee}.

It follows that
L_{\sigma}:L_{R}\to L_{R^{\prime}}
induces a surjection
L_{R}/L_{R}^{(2)}\to L_{R^{\prime}}/L_{R^{\prime}}^{(2)}
. Now Lemma
6.1
(i) implies that
L_{\sigma}
is surjective.

In the reverse direction note that the maps
id_{L}
and
E\to 0
define a retraction
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,E\to L
. The corresponding retraction
L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,E^{\prime}\to L^{\prime}
fits in the commutative diagram,

\textstyle{L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\,\widehat{\amalg}\,g}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,E^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}.}

Therefore if
f\,\widehat{\amalg}\,g
is surjective so is
f
and, similarly, so is
g
.

\square

Example.
Free products of completions of weighted Lie algebras

Let
E
and
E^{\prime}
be weighted enriched Lie algebras with enriched structures given by morphisms
E\to E_{\alpha}
and
E^{\prime}\to E_{\beta}^{\prime}
.
Denote by
L
and
L^{\prime}
the completions of
E
and
E^{\prime}
. The weight decompositions in
E
and
E^{\prime}
then extend in the standard way to a weight decomposition in
E\,\amalg\,E^{\prime}
.
It then follows from §2.1 that
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime}
is the completion of
E\,\amalg\,E^{\prime}
.

10.3
Limits and colimits of complete enriched Lie algebras

Proposition 10.4
.

The category
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
of complete enriched Lie algebras is complete and cocomplete : i.e., each diagram of complete enriched Lie algebras has a direct and an inverse limit.

proof.
The construction of the coproduct of two complete enriched Lie algebras can be generalized to any family
\{L(i)\}_{i\in I}
of complete enriched Lie algebras. In fact, denote by
A_{i}
the family of ideals defining the enriched structure in
L(i)
. For each finite subset
J\subset I
, we denote by
p_{J}:\amalg_{i\in I}L(i)\to\amalg_{i\in J}L(i)
the projection defined by
p_{J}(L(i))=0
for
i\not\in J
. Then for
J\subset I
, finite, for
\alpha_{i}\in A_{i}
for
i\in J
, and for an integer
n
, we denote by
K_{J,(\alpha_{i}),n}
the kernel of the projection

\textstyle{\amalg_{i\in I}L(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{J}}
\textstyle{\amalg_{i\in J}L(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\amalg_{i\in J}L(i)_{\alpha_{i}}/(\amalg_{i\in J}L(i)_{\alpha_{i}})^{n}.}

These ideals define an enriched structure on
\amalg_{i\in I}L(i)
and its completion
\overline{\amalg_{i\in I}L(i)}
is the coproduct of the the
L(i)
is the category of complete enriched Lie algebras.

Now let
(L(i),f_{j})
be a diagram in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. Its colimit in the category of Lie algebras is the quotient of
\amalg_{i\in I}L(i)
by an ideal
J
associated to the morphisms
f_{j}
. Denote by
\iota:\amalg_{i\in I}L(i)\to\overline{\amalg_{i\in I}L(i)}
the natural injection and by
\overline{J}
the closure of the ideal generated by
\iota(J)
in
\overline{\amalg L(i)}
. Then by the Corollary to Lemma
2.2
, the quotient
\overline{\amalg L(i)}/\overline{J}
inherits a complete enriched structure. This is clearly the colimit of the diagram
(L(i),f_{j})
in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.

On the other hand the usual product of the complete enriched Lie algebras
L(i)
is complete for the family of the ideals which are the kernels of the surjections

\textstyle{\prod_{i\in I}L(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{J}}
\textstyle{\prod_{i\in J}L(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod\rho(i)_{\alpha_{i}}}
\textstyle{L(i)_{\alpha_{i}}}

associated to the finite subsets
J\subset I
and the surjections
\rho(i)_{\alpha_{i}}:L(i)\to L(i)_{\alpha_{i}}.

Now consider a diagram
(L(i),f_{j})
in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. Its limit in the category of Lie algebras is the sub Lie algebra
S\subset\prod L(i)
consisting of families
a_{i}\in L(i)
such that for any morphism
f_{j}:L(i_{0})\to L(i_{1})
we have
f_{j}(a_{i_{0}})=a_{i_{1}}
. Since for any morphism
f:L(1)\to L(2)
of complete enriched Lie algebras,
f
is a limit of morphisms
f_{\alpha}:L(1)_{\beta(\alpha)}\to L(2)_{\alpha}
, the sub Lie algebra
S
is closed and is the limit of the diagram in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.

\square

10.4
Wedges of spheres

Suppose
\{S^{n_{\alpha}}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{S}}
is a collection of spheres in which
\mathcal{S}
is a linearly ordered set and each
n_{\alpha}\geq 1
. For each finite subset
\sigma\subset{\mathcal{S}}
we write
\sigma=\{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{r}\}
with
\sigma_{1}<\dots<\sigma_{r}
, and set
|\sigma|=r
. If
\sigma\subset\tau
, so that
|\sigma|\leq|\tau|=q
, the inclusion defines an inclusion

j_{\sigma,\tau}:S^{n_{\sigma_{1}}}\vee\dots\vee S^{n_{\sigma_{r}}}\longrightarrow S^{n_{\tau_{1}}}\vee\dots\vee S^{n_{\tau_{q}}}.

Definition.
A
wedge of spheres
is a direct limit of the form

X=\vee_{\alpha}S^{n_{\alpha}}:=\varinjlim_{\sigma_{1}<\dots<\sigma_{r}}\,\,S^{n_{\sigma_{1}}}\vee\dots\vee S^{n_{\sigma_{r}}}.

Remark.
It is immediate from the definition that a wedge of spheres is a co-H-space. Thus (Corollary 3 to Proposition 8.4) its minimal Sullivan model,
\land V
, is quadratic and its homotopy Lie algebra is profree. Thus we may assume
d:V\to\land^{2}V
and write
V=S\oplus T
with

S=V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{\geq 1}(\land V).

Now let
{\mathcal{S}}=\{\alpha\}
index a linearly ordered basis
\{v_{\alpha}\}
of
S
. This then defines a wedge

X=\vee_{\alpha}S^{n_{\alpha}}

of spheres.

Proposition 10.5
.

A minimal Sullivan algebra,
\land V
, is the Sullivan model of a wedge of spheres,
X
, if and only if

(i)

The homotopy Lie algebra,
L_{V}
, is profree, and

(ii)

H(\land V)
is the dual of a graded vector space.

Moreover, if
H(\land V)
is a graded vector space of finite type then
\land V
determines
X
up to homeomorphism.

proof.
As observed above a wedge of spheres,
X
, has a profree homotopy Lie algebra. Moreover
H(\land V)=H(X)=H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}
.

In the reverse direction suppose
\land V
satisfies (i) and (ii), so that
L_{V}
is profree and
H^{\geq 1}(\land V)=T^{\vee}
. Then by Proposition
8.4
,

\land V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathbb{Q}\oplus T^{\vee},0).

Choose a linearly ordered basis
\{x_{i}\}
of
T
and set
n_{i}=\mbox{deg}\,x_{i}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{X}=\{i\}
.
Then set

X=\vee_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{X}}S^{n_{i}}_{i}.

By construction,
H_{\geq 1}(X;\mathbb{Q})=T.

Now let
\land W
be the minimal Sullivan model of
X
. Since
X
is a wedge of spheres,

\land W\simeq(\mathbb{Q}\oplus H^{\geq 1}(X),0)=(\mathbb{Q}\oplus T^{\vee},0)=(\mathbb{Q}\oplus H^{\geq 1}(\land V),0)\simeq\land V.

Since
\land W
and
\land V
are minimal Sullivan algebras, it follows that
\land V\cong\land W
. Therefore
\land V
is the minimal Sullivan model of
X
.

Finally, suppose
H(\land V)
is a graded vector space of finite type, and that
\land V
is the minimal Sullivan model of a second wedge of spheres
Y
. Then
H^{\geq 1}(Y)
and
H^{\geq 1}(X)
are isomorphic vetor spaces and so
H_{\geq 1}(Y;\mathbb{Q})\cong H_{\geq 1}(X;\mathbb{Q})
. Since
Y
is a wedge of spheres

Y=\vee_{j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{Y}}S^{n_{j}}

and the number of indices
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{Y}
with
n_{j}=n
is dim
\,H_{n}(Y;\mathbb{Q})=
dim
\,H_{n}(X;\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore
{\mathcal{S}}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{Y}
are isomorphic sets and
X\cong Y
.
\square

These constructions provide an explicit description of the morphism (§9),

\pi_{*}|\varphi|:\pi_{*}(Y)\to\pi_{*}(Y_{\mathbb{Q}})

when
Y=\vee_{\sigma}S^{n_{\sigma}}
is a wedge of spheres of finite type. When
Y
is a wedge of circles, this extends diagrams (1) and (2) in §9.1.

Proposition 10.6
.

With the hypotheses and notation above, denote by
\sigma\in\pi_{n_{\sigma}}(S^{n_{\sigma}})
the homotopy class represented by id
{}_{S^{n_{\sigma}}}
. Then the homotopy Lie algebra
L_{Y}
of
Y
can be written
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
in which
\pi_{*}|\varphi|
maps the set
\{\sigma\}
bijectively to a basis of
T
.

proof.
Here (Proposition
8.4
)
L_{Y}=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
with
sT=(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}
. Because
Y
is a wedge of finite type,

(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}=H^{\geq 1}(Y)^{\vee}=H_{\geq 1}(Y;\mathbb{Q}).

However, a basis of
H_{\geq 1}(Y;\mathbb{Q})
is provided by the images of the homotopy classes
\sigma
under the Hurewicz homomorphism. Since the Hurewicz homomorphism is natural, the Proposition follows from Proposition
9.3
.
\square

Definition.
A space
X
is
rationally wedge-like
if it is homotopy equivalent to a spatial realization
\langle\land V\rangle
with the homotopy Lie algebra of
\land V
a profree Lie algebra.

Remark.
It follows from Proposition
10.5
that if
\vee S^{n}_{\alpha}=X
is a wedge of spheres and
\land V
is its minimal Sullivan model then

(\vee S^{n}_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{Q}}=X_{\mathbb{Q}}=\langle\land V\rangle

is rationally wedge-like.

11
Sullivan rational spaces

Let
\varphi_{X}:\land V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}A_{PL}(X)
be a minimal Sullivan model for a connected space,
X
, and let
L_{X}
denote the homotopy Lie algebra of
\land V
. Recall (§9) that the adjoint map

|\varphi_{X}|:X\to\langle\land V\rangle:=X_{\mathbb{Q}}

is called the
Sullivan completion
of
X
.

Definition.
A path connected space,
X
, is a
Sullivan rational space
if the Sullivan completion
|\varphi_{X}|:X\to X_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a homotopy equivalence.

Remarks. 1.
The condition that
|\varphi_{X}|
is a homotopy equivalence implies that the minimal Sullivan model of
X
directly computes both
H(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
and
\pi_{*}(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

2.
If
X
is Sullivan rational then
X_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq(X_{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
is Sullivan rational.

Recall that (
[
40
]
) Quillen’s rationalization for simply connected spaces assigns to each such space a map
Y\to\mathbb{Q}(Y)
which induces isomorphisms

H_{\geq 1}(Y)\otimes\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H_{\geq 1}(\mathbb{Q}(Y))\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }\pi_{*}(Y)\otimes\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\pi_{*}(\mathbb{Q}(Y)).

In particular,
\mathbb{Q}(\mathbb{Q}(Y))=\mathbb{Q}(Y)
.

Sullivan’s completion,
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
, is analogous to Quillen’s rationalization, and also to the Bousfield-Kan completion
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}(X)
- cf
[
7
]
. Moreover, if
H(X)
has finite type then
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}(X)
and
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
are homotopy equivalent (
[
6
]
). But it is not always true that every
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
is Sullivan rational. For instance (
[
28
]
),
H^{2}((S^{1}\vee S^{1})_{\mathbb{Q}})
has uncountable dimension, where as if
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
is Sullivan rational by the Corollary to Theorem
2
below,
H(X_{\mathbb{Q}})
has finite type.

Remarks

1.
If
\varphi_{X}:\land V\to A_{PL}(X)
is a minimal Sullivan model then
X
is Sullivan rational if and only if each

\pi_{k}|\varphi_{X}|:(V^{k})^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi_{k}(X).

2.
Let
Y
be a Sullivan rational space and let
X
be a connected space. Since
f_{\mathbb{Q}}\circ\varphi_{X}=\varphi_{Y}\circ f
, and
\varphi_{Y}
is a homotopy equivalence, it follows that
f\mapsto f_{\mathbb{Q}}
yields an injection

[X,Y]\to[X_{\mathbb{Q}},Y_{\mathbb{Q}}].

Now recall from §7 that if
\land V
is a Sullivan algebra then adjoint to
id_{\langle\land V\rangle}
is a morphism

\iota_{\land V}:\land V\to A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle.

Lemma 11.1
.

Suppose for some minimal Sullivan algebra,
\land V
, that
\iota_{\land V}:\land V\to A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle
is a quasi-isomorphism. Then

(i)

\langle\land V\rangle
is Sullivan rational.

(ii)

A basis of
V
is at most countable.

(iii)

H(\land V)
is a graded vector space of finite type.

proof.
(i) By hypothesis
\iota_{\land V}
is a Sullivan model for
\langle\land V\rangle
. Since its adjoint is the homotopy equivalence
id_{\langle\land V\rangle}
,
\langle\land V\rangle
is Sullivan rational.

(ii). It follows from (17) that the
surjection
V^{\vee}\to(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee}
decomposes as the composite

\textstyle{V^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
hur
\textstyle{H_{*}\langle\land V\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee}.}

Thus
H_{*}\langle\land V\rangle\to(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee}
is surjective. Dualizing gives injections
(V^{n}\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee\vee}\to H^{n}(\land V).

Now assume by induction that
V^{<n}
has an at most countable basis and let
Z
be the space of cycles in
(\land V)^{n}
. Then
Z\subset\land V^{<n}\oplus V^{n}
, and division by
V^{n}\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
gives an exact sequence

0\to V^{n}\cap\mbox{ker}\,d\to Z\to\land V^{<n}.

Since
(V^{n}\cap\mbox{ker}\,d)^{\vee\vee}
embeds in
H^{n}(\land V)
, it follows that dim
\,V^{n}\cap\mbox{ker}\,d<\infty
.

On the other hand,
V^{n}=\cup_{p\geq 0}V^{n}_{p}
with
V^{n}_{0}=V^{n}\cap d^{-1}(\land V^{<n})
and
V_{p+1}^{n}=V^{n}\cap d^{-1}(\land V^{<n}\otimes\land V^{n}_{p})
. Since dim
\,V^{n}\cap\mbox{ker}\,d<\infty
, it follows by induction on
p
that each
V^{n}_{p}
, and hence
V^{n}
itself, has an at most countable basis.

(iii) It follows from (i) that each
H^{n}(\land V)
has an at most countable basis. But since
H^{n}(\land V)=H^{n}(\langle\land V\rangle)=H_{n}\langle\land V\rangle^{\vee}
, this implies that each dim
\,H^{n}(\land V)<\infty
.
\square

Lemma 11.2
.

Suppose
\land V
is a minimal Sullivan algebra. If
V^{1}=0
then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

\iota_{\land V}:\land V\to A_{PL}\langle\land V\rangle
is a quasi-isomorphism.

(ii)

Each dim
\,V^{k}<\infty
.

(iii)

Each dim
\,H^{k}(\land V)<\infty.

(iv)

Each dim
\,\pi_{k}<\land V>
is finite.

proof.
(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii). This is
[
19
, Theorem 1.5]

(ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii). This follows because
d:V^{k}\to\land V^{<k}
.

(ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv) This is because
\pi_{k}=(V^{k})^{\vee}
.
\square

Lemma 11.3
.

Suppose
G
is a discrete group and its classifying space,
BG
, is Sullivan rational. Then

(i)

Each
G^{n}/G^{n+1}
is a finite dimensional rational vector space.

(ii)

G=\varprojlim_{n}G/G^{n}
.

proof.
Let
\land V
be the minimal Sullivan model of
BG
. Then

(V^{n})^{\vee}=\pi_{n}\langle\land V\rangle=\pi_{n}(BG)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&n\geq 2\\
G&n=1\end{array},\right.

Thus
V=V^{1}
, and (cf Proposition 8.2)

G=\pi_{1}\langle\land V^{1}\rangle=G_{L}

where
L=L_{0}
is the homotopy Lie algebra of
\land V
. Now the Lemma follows from Proposition
8.2
.
\square

Proposition 11.1
.

Suppose
\varphi:\land V\to A_{PL}(X)
is a Sullivan model of a connected space,
X
. Then

(i)

H(|\varphi|):H(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longleftarrow}}H(X_{\mathbb{Q}})\,\Longleftrightarrow\,H(\iota_{\land V}):H(\land V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}H(\langle\land V\rangle)
.

(ii)

When the conditions of (i) hold then
\langle\land V\rangle
is Sullivan rational,
X_{\mathbb{Q}}\to(X_{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
is an isomorphism of simplicial sets, and
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
is Sullivan rational.

proof.
(i) According to (
15
),

\varphi=A_{PL}(|\varphi|)\circ\iota_{\land V}.

Since
H(\varphi)
is an isomorphism this establishes (i).

(ii) By definition,
X_{\mathbb{Q}}=\langle\land V\rangle
. Therefore (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) follows from Lemma
11.1
.
\square

Now suppose that

\textstyle{\land W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{B}}
\textstyle{\land W\otimes\land Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\land Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{F}}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A_{PL}(p)}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A_{PL}(j)}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(F)}

be a commutative cdga diagram, in which

\bullet

The upper row is a
\Lambda
-extension decomposition of a minimal Sullivan algebra
\land V=\land W\otimes\land Z
.

\bullet

The lower row is an application of
A_{PL}(-)
to a fibration
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
of path connected spaces.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose in (
11
) that
F
is Sullivan rational and that
H(\varphi_{F})
is an isomorphism. Then

(i)

The holonomy representation of
\pi_{1}(B)
in each
H^{k}(F)
is nilpotent.

(ii)

H(\varphi_{B})
is an isomorphism
\Leftrightarrow

H(\varphi_{X})
is an isomorphism.

(iii)

If
H(\varphi_{B})
and
H(\varphi_{X})
are isomorphisms then
B
and
X
are Sullivan rational.

proof.
(i) The adjoint map
\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle:B\to\langle\land W\rangle
induces a morphism of fundamental groups, and (
[
19
, Theorem 4.2]
) the isomorphism
H(\varphi_{F}):H^{k}(\land Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H^{k}(F)
is equivariant with respect to the two corresponding holonomy representations. The first is locally nilpotent (
[
19
, Proposition 4.4]
) and the second is the dual of a representation of
\pi_{1}(F)
in
H_{*}(F)
. It follows that both representations are nilpotent.

(ii) Since
\land Z
is the minimal Sullivan model of a Sullivan rational space it follows from Lemma
11.1
that
H(\land Z)
is a graded vector space of finite type. Thus, if
H(\varphi_{B})
is an isomorphism, (
[
19
, Theorem 5.1]
) provides a Sullivan algebra
(\land V^{\prime},d^{\prime})=(\land W\otimes\land Z,d^{\prime})
and a commutative cdga diagram

\textstyle{\land W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(\land W\otimes\land Z,d^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\land Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A_{PL}(p)}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(F).}

It is straightforward to extend the inclusion of
\land W
to a lift of
\varphi_{X}
through
\varphi^{\prime}
and to conclude that
H(\varphi_{X})
is an isomorphism.

On the other hand, suppose
H(\varphi_{X})
is an isomorphism. Then extend
\varphi_{B}
to a quasi-isomorphism,
\varphi_{B}^{\prime}:\land W\otimes\land W^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{PL}(B)
, from a minimal
\Lambda
-extension. By
[
19
, Theorem 5.1]
this extends to a commutative cdga diagram

\textstyle{\land W\otimes\land W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\land W\otimes\land W^{\prime}\otimes\land Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\land Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{F}}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{PL}(F).}

Since
\varphi_{X}:\land W\otimes\land Z\to A_{PL}(X)
is also a quasi-isomorphism it follows that
W^{\prime}=0
and
H(\varphi_{B})
is an isomorphism.

(iii) In (
11
) we are now assuming that each of
H(\varphi_{B})
,
H(\varphi_{X})
and
H(\varphi_{F})
are isomorphisms. On the other hand taking the adjoints of
\varphi_{B}
,
\varphi_{X}
and
\varphi_{F}
produces the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\langle\land W\rangle}
\textstyle{\langle\land W\otimes\land Z\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\langle\land Z\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\varphi_{X}\rangle}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\langle\varphi_{F}\rangle}

According to (
[
17
, Proposition 17.9]
) the upper row is also a fibration. Thus it follows from the 5-lemma that if any two of
\pi_{*}\langle\varphi_{B}\rangle
,
\pi_{*}\langle\varphi_{X}\rangle
and
\pi_{*}\langle\varphi_{F}\rangle
are isomorphisms so is the third.
\square

Proposition 11.2
.

If
\land V
is a Sullivan algebra and each dim
\,V^{l}<\infty
then

\iota_{\land V}

is a quasi-isomorphism.

proof.
First observe that if deg
\,v=1
then

[S^{k},\langle\land v\rangle]=[\land v,A_{PL}(S^{k})]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{Q}&\mbox{if }k=1\\
0&\mbox{if }k>1.\end{array}\right.

Thus
\pi_{k}\langle\land v\rangle=\mathbb{Q}
if
k=1
and
0
if
k>1
. It follows that

H^{k}(\langle\land v\rangle)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{Q}&\mbox{if }k=1\\
0&\mbox{if }k>1\end{array}\right.

Moreover the identity of
\langle\land v\rangle
is adjoint to
\iota_{\land v}
and so
\iota_{\land v}
is not null homotopic; Therefore

H(\iota_{\land v}):\land v\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{PL}\langle\land v\rangle.

Now we proceed by induction on dim
V^{1}
. If
V^{1}=0
the Proposition is proved in (
[
19
, Theorem 1.5]
). Otherwise we have a
\Lambda
-extension decomposition of the form
\land V=\land v\otimes\land W
. In this case the Proposition follows from Theorem
2
.
\square

Part IV
Enriched dgl’s and their homotopy theory

12
Enriched dgl’s and semi-quadratic Sullivan algebras

Definitions.

(1)

A
differential graded Lie algebra
(dgl),
(L,\partial)
is a graded Lie algebra
L=\{L_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
together with a differential
\partial:L_{k}\to L_{k-1}
which is also a right derivation:

\displaystyle\partial^{2}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\mbox{and }\partial[x,y]=(-1)^{deg\,y}[\partial x,y]+[x,\partial y].

(34)

(2)

An
enriched dgl
(edgl) is a dgl,
(L,\partial)
, together with an inverse system of morphisms
\rho_{\alpha}:(L,\partial)\to(L_{\alpha},\partial_{\alpha})
such that

\bullet

each
(L_{\alpha},\partial_{\alpha})
is a finite dimensional nilpotent dgl, and

\bullet

L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varprojlim_{\alpha}L_{\alpha}
.

In particular,
L
is a complete enriched Lie algebra
and
\partial
is a coherent linear map.

(3)

An enriched dgl,
(L,\partial)
is
minimal
if
\partial:L\to L^{(2)}
.

(4)

A
morphism of enriched dgl’s
is a morphism of enriched Lie algebras which commutes with the differentials. In particular, morphisms are coherent. We denote the set of morphisms
(L,\partial)\to(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})
by
\mbox{dgl}((L,\partial),(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime}))
.

(5)

A
dgl ideal
in an enriched dgl,
L
, is a closed differential ideal; in which case (Lemma
1.1
)
L/I
is an enriched dgl.

Remarks.

1)

For simplicity we will frequently denote an enriched dgl simply by
(L,\partial)
.

2)

The convention that
\partial
be a right derivation arises from the fact that in applications
L
frequently acts from the right.

The 1-1 correspondence in §4 between complete enriched Lie algebras and quadratic Sullivan algebras extends to enriched dgl’s,
(L,\partial)
as follows. First, let
(\land V,d_{1})
be the quadratic Sullivan model of
L
. Recall from §4 that the inverse limit
sL=\varprojlim_{\alpha}sL_{\alpha}
is the dual of a unique direct limit
V=\varinjlim_{\alpha}V_{\alpha}
, where
(\land V_{\alpha},d_{\alpha})=C^{*}(L_{\alpha})
. Then

(\land V,d_{1})=\varinjlim_{\alpha}\,(\land V_{\alpha},d_{1,\alpha})

is by definition the quadratic model of
L
.

On the other hand, each
\partial_{\alpha}
is the dual (up to suspension) of a unique differential
d_{0,\alpha}
in
V_{\alpha}
and, by Proposition
2.1
,
\partial
is the dual of the differential
d_{0}=\varinjlim_{\alpha}d_{0,\alpha}
in
V
:

<d_{0}v,sx>=<v,s\partial x>,\hskip 28.45274ptv\in V,x\in L.

Finally, we extend
d_{0}
to a derivation in
\land V_{0}
. Now a minimal Sullivan model
(\land W,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}(\land V,d_{0})
induces an isomorphism
W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}H(V,d_{0})
. Let
L_{W}
denote the homotopy Lie algebra of
\land W
. Then there are natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{l}s(L,\partial)=\varprojlim_{\alpha}s(L_{\alpha},\partial_{\alpha})=\left[\varinjlim_{\alpha}(V_{\alpha},d_{0,\alpha}\right]^{\vee}=(V,d_{0})^{\vee}\\
H(L,\partial)=s^{-1}H(V,d_{0})^{\vee}=s^{-1}W^{\vee}=L_{W},\end{array}\right\}

which in particular provide an isomorphism
H(L,\partial)\cong L_{W}
of complete enriched Lie algebras. It also follows from (
34
) that
(d_{0}+d_{1})^{2}=0
. Thus Lemma
12.1
(i) immediately below shows that
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
is a Sullivan algebra.

Definition.
(i) A
semi-quadratic Sullivan algebra
is a Sullivan algebra of the form
(\land V,d=d_{0}+d_{1})
in which
d_{0}:V\to V
and
d_{1}:V\to\land^{2}V
.

(ii) The semi-quadratic Sullivan algebra corresponding to an enriched dgl
L
is its
semi-quadratic Sullivan model
, and is denoted
\land V_{L}
.

(iii) A cdga morphism
\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})
is
bihomogeneous
if
\varphi:V\to W
.

Lemma 12.1
.

Let
(\land W,d_{0}+d)
be a cdga in which
d_{0}:W\to W
,
d:W\to\land^{\geq 2}W
and
d^{2}=0
. Then
(\land W,d_{0}+d)
is a Sullivan algebra if and only if
(\land W,d)
is a Sullivan algebra.

proof.
Suppose first that
(\land W,d_{0}+d)
is a Sullivan algebra. Then
\land W=\varinjlim_{n}\land W_{n}
in which
d_{0}+d:W_{n+1}\to\land W_{n}
. But this implies that
d:W_{n+1}\to\land W_{n}
and so
(\land W,d)
is a Sullivan algebra.

In the reverse direction, suppose
(\land W,d)
is a Sullivan algebra. If
P\subset W
is finite dimensional then
P\subset
some
W^{\prime}
with dim
\,W^{\prime}<\infty
and
\land W^{\prime}
preserved by
d
. It follows that
\land(W^{\prime}+d_{0}W^{\prime})
is preserved by both
d
and
d_{0}
. Therefore it is sufficient to prove that
(\land W,d_{0}+d)
is a Sullivan algebra when dim
\,W<\infty
.

In this case we set
W_{0}=W\cap\mbox{ker}\,d
. Then
\land W
decomposes as the
\Lambda
-extension

(\land W_{0},d_{0})\to(\land W_{0}\otimes\land Z,d_{0}+d)\to(\land Z,\overline{d_{0}}+\overline{d})

of cdga’s and the result follows by induction on dim
\,W
.
\square

Proposition 12.1
.

(i)

The correspondence
(L,\partial)\leadsto(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
is a bijection between enriched dgl’s and semi-quadratic Sullivan algebras.

(ii)

This correspondence identifies the morphisms
f:(L,\partial)\to(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})
with the bihomogeneous morphisms
\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\leftarrow(\land V^{\prime},d_{0}^{\prime}+d_{1}^{\prime})
via

\mbox{suspension of }f=(\varphi|_{V^{\prime}})^{\vee}.

In particular
\varphi:(\land V,d_{1})\leftarrow(\land V^{\prime},d_{1}^{\prime})
is the morphism of quadratic Sullivan algebras corresponding to
f:L\to L^{\prime}
.

(iii)

(L,\partial)
is minimal if and only if
d_{0}(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})=0
.

(iv)

If
I\subset L
is a dgl ideal in an enriched dgl,
L
, then the morphisms
\land V_{L/I}\to\land V_{L}\to\land V_{I}
decompose
\land V_{L}
as a Sullivan extension

\land V_{L}=\land V_{L/I}\otimes\land V_{I}.

Conversely, if an inclusion
\land W\to\land V_{L}
of semi-quadratic Sullivan algebras decomposes
\land V_{L}
as a Sullivan extension
\land W\otimes\land Z
then the enriched dgl corresponding to
\land Z
is a dgl ideal
I\subset L
and
\land W=\land V_{L/I}
.

proof.

(i) This is immediate.

(ii) The correspondence associates to a morphism
f
the map
\varphi:V\leftarrow V^{\prime}
, via the equation

<\varphi v^{\prime},sx>=<v^{\prime},sf(x)>.

In this case
\varphi\circ d_{0}=d_{0}^{\prime}\circ\varphi
and
\varphi\circ d_{1}=d_{1}^{\prime}\circ\varphi
. The bijection is a straightforward consequence of the definitions and Propositions
2.1
and
4.1
.

(iii) This follows because (Lemma
4.1
) the isomorphism
sL\cong V^{\vee}
induces an isomorphism
s(L/L^{(2)})\cong(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1})^{\vee}
.

(iv) This is immediate from the definitions and §4.1.
\square

Definition.
If
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
is the semi-quadratic Sullivan algebra corresponding to an enriched dgl
(L,\partial)
then it is the
semi-quadratic Sullivan model
of
(L,\partial)
and
(L,\partial)
is the
enriched dgl model
of
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
.

Remark.
In working with enriched dgl’s and their semi-quadratic models it is important to note that

1)

Not every morphism is bihomogeneous, and

2)

The semi-quadratic Sullivan model of a minimal enriched dgl is a Sullivan algebra, but is not minimal unless
d_{0}=0
.

Finally, if
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
is a semi-quadratic Sullivan algebra then it is immediate that
\land V(n):=\land V^{\leq n}\otimes\land d_{0}V^{n}
is preserved by both
d_{0}
and
d_{1}
. Thus

(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})=\varinjlim_{n}\,(\land V(n),d_{0}+d_{1}).

Proposition 12.2
.

(i)

A bihomogeneous morphism
\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})
of semi-quadratic Sullivan algebras restricts to morphisms

\varphi(n):(\land V(n),d_{0}+d_{1})\to(\land W(n),d_{0}+d_{1}),\hskip 14.22636ptn\geq 0.

(ii)

If
\psi\sim\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})
then

\varphi(n)\sim\psi(n),\hskip 14.22636ptn\geq 0.

(iii)

A bihomogeneous morphism
\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if each
\varphi(n)
is a quasi-isomorphism.

proof.
(i) Since
\varphi
preserves degrees it maps
\land V^{\leq n}
to
\land W^{\leq n}
. But if
v\in V^{n}
then

\varphi(d_{0}v)=\varphi(dv-d_{1}v)=d\varphi v-\varphi d_{1}v=d_{0}\varphi v+d_{1}\varphi v-\varphi d_{1}v\in W(n),

and so
\varphi:V(n)\to W(n)
.

(ii) This follows in the same way as (i).

(iii) Since
\varphi
is a quasi-isomorphism of Sullivan algebras it has a homotopy inverse
\widehat{\varphi}:\land V\leftarrow\land W
. It follows from (i) and (ii) that
\widehat{\varphi}
and
\varphi
restrict to homotopy inverses between each
V(n)
and
W(n)
.

\square

12.1
Homology of a semi-quadratic Sullivan algebra

Lemma 12.2
.

If
(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})
is a semi-quadratic Sullivan algebra then

(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})=\varinjlim_{\alpha}(\land W_{\alpha},d_{0}+d_{1})

is the limit of sub semi-quadratic Sullivan algebras
(W_{\alpha}\subset W)
for which each
H(W_{\alpha},d_{0})\to H(W,d_{0})
is injective.

proof.
A sub cdga
(\land W_{\beta},d_{0}+d)
of
\land W
will be called
regular
if dim
\,W_{\beta}<\infty
and if

H(W_{\beta},d_{0})\to H(W,d_{0})

is injective. We show by induction on
n
that any finite dimensional subspace
P\subset W^{\leq n}
is contained in a regular cdga of the form
(\land V^{\leq n}\oplus d_{0}V^{n}).

Assuming this for some
n
, let
P\subset W^{\leq n+1}
be finite dimensional. It follows from (i) that
P\subset Z=Z^{\leq n+1}+d_{0}(Z^{\leq n+1})
, where dim
\,Z<\infty
and
\land Z
is preserved by
d_{0}
and
d_{1}
. Now choose a subspace
S\subset W^{n}
so that
S\cap Z^{n}=0
and

0\to S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{0}}}{{\to}}H^{n+1}(Z,d_{0})\to H^{n+1}(W,d_{0})

is exact. By our induction hypothesis there is a regular cdga of the form
\land(V^{\leq n}\oplus d_{0}V^{n})
and for which

S\oplus Z^{\leq n}\subset V^{\leq n}.

Now, set
V^{n+1}=Z^{n+1}+d_{0}V^{n}
. We show that
\land(V^{\leq n+1}\oplus d_{0}V^{n+1})
is regular. Since
P\subset W^{\leq n+1}
this will complete the proof.

But since
\land Z
and
\land(V^{\leq n}\oplus d_{0}V^{n})
are both preserved by
d_{0}
and by
d_{1}
it follows that so is
\land(V^{\leq n+1}\oplus d_{0}V^{n+1})
. Moreover, for
k\leq n
the map
H^{k}(V^{\leq n+1}\oplus d_{0}V^{n+1})\to H^{k}(W)
coincides with the injection
H^{k}(V^{\leq n}\oplus d_{0}V^{n})\to H^{k}(W)
. Also, a cycle in
V^{n+1}
is homologous to a cycle in
Z^{n+1}
. Thus if it is a boundary in
W^{n+1}
, it is in
d_{0}(S)
and hence a boundary in
W^{n+1}
.

\square

Corollary.
If
(L,\partial)
is complete and dim
\,H(L)/[H(L),H(L)]<\infty
then
H(L)
is pronilpotent.

proof.
This follows directly from Proposition
3.1
.

\square

Proposition 12.3
.

The enriched structure in an enriched dgl can be represented by surjections
\rho_{\beta}:(L,\partial)\to(L_{\beta},\partial_{\beta})
for which
(H(L),\{H(\rho_{\beta})\})
is a complete enriched Lie algebra.

proof.
An enriched structure in
(L,\partial)
is represented by surjections
\rho_{\beta}:L\to L_{\beta}
which are dual to inclusions
j_{\beta}:(V_{\beta},d_{0}+d_{1})\to(V,d_{0}+d_{1})
. By Lemma
12.2
we may assume that each
H(j_{\beta}):H(V_{\beta},d_{0})\to H(V,d_{0})
is injective. Then
H(\rho_{\beta})=s^{-1}H(j_{\beta})^{\vee}
is surjective and
(H(L),\{H(\rho_{\beta})\})
is an enriched Lie algebra.
Since
L
is complete,
(L,\partial)=\varprojlim_{\beta}(L_{\beta},\partial_{\beta})
. Thus it follows from Proposition
1.1
that
H(L)=\varprojlim\,H(L_{\beta})
is complete.
\square

Proposition 12.4
.

Suppose
\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\leftarrow(\land V^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{0}+d^{\prime}_{1})
is the bihomogeneous morphism corresponding to a morphism
f:(L,\partial)\to(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})
of enriched dgl’s. Then

(i)

H(\varphi,d_{0}):(\land H(V,d_{0}),H(d_{1}))\leftarrow(\land H(V^{\prime},d_{0}^{\prime}),H(d_{1}))
is the bihomogeneous morphism corresponding to
H(f):(H(L),0)\to(H(L^{\prime}),0)
.

(ii)

H(f)
is an isomorphism
\Leftrightarrow

H(\varphi,d_{0})
is an isomorphism.

(iii)

If
H(\varphi,d_{0})
is an isomorphism then
H(\varphi,d_{0}+d_{1})
is an isomorphism.

proof.
(i) It follows from (
12
) that
H(\varphi,d_{0})^{\vee}
is the suspension of
H(f):L_{V}\to L_{V^{\prime}}
, where
L_{V}
and
L_{V^{\prime}}
are the homotopy Lie algebras of
\land H(V,d_{0})
and
\land H(V^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{0})
.

(ii) This is immediate from (
12
).

(iii) Filtering by wedge degree produces a spectral sequence converging from
H(\land V,d_{0})
to
H(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
, and this establishes (ii).
\square

Proposition 12.5
.

Suppose
\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\to(\land W,D)
is a quasi-isomorphism from a semi-quadratic Sullivan algebra to a minimal Sullivan algebra.

(i)

Then
\varphi
is surjective and restricts to surjective dga morphisms

(\land^{\geq k}V,d_{0}+d_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land^{\geq k}W,D).

(ii)

The morphism of wedge-filtration spectral sequences induced by
\varphi
begins with the isomorphism of
E_{1}
-terms

(\land H(V,d_{0}),H(d_{1}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land W,D_{1}),

where
D_{1}
is the quadratic component of
D
.

(iii)

The isomorphism, (ii), of quadratic Sullivan algebras identifies the isomorphism

H(L,\partial)\cong L_{W}
of (
12
) as an isomorphism of enriched Lie algebras.

proof.
(i) Since
\varphi
is a quasi-isomorphism of Sullivan algebras it has a homotopy inverse,
\psi:(\land W,D)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
. Thus
\varphi\circ\psi
is a quasi-isomorphism of minimal Sullivan algebras, and is therefore an isomorphism. In particular it restricts to dga automorphisms of each
\land^{\geq k}W
. It follows that
\varphi
restricts to surjective dga morphisms
\land^{\geq k}V\to\land^{\geq k}W
,
k\geq 0
.

(ii) Decompose
V
as

V=Z\oplus S\oplus d_{0}S,

in which ker
\,d_{0}=Z\oplus d_{0}S
and
d_{0}:S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}d_{0}S
. Filtration by wedge degree then shows that the inclusions
Z,S
and
d_{0}S
in
\land V
extend to an isomorphism

\chi:\land Z\otimes\land S\otimes\land d_{0}S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\land V

of graded algebras. It follows that a differential,
d_{Z}
, in
\land Z
is determined by the requirement that
\chi
be a dga isomorphism. Since
\varphi
is a quasi-isomorphism its restriction,
\chi_{Z}
, to
(\land Z,d_{Z})
is a quasi-isomorphism of minimal Sullivan algebras, and hence an isomorphism.

On the other hand
\chi
also induces a morphism of the spectral sequences induced by filtration by wedge degrees, and the
E_{0}
-term is an isomorphism

(\land Z,0)\otimes(\land(S\oplus d_{0}S),d_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land V,d_{0}).

Thus the
E_{1}
- morphism is an isomorphism

(\land Z,(d_{Z})_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\land H_{0}(V),H(d_{1})).

Finally, filtering
\land Z
and
\land W
by wedge degrees converts
\chi_{Z}
into a morphism of spectral sequences for which the
E_{1}
-term is an isomorphism
(\land Z,(d_{Z})_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}(\land W,D_{1})
. This, combined with the isomorphism above, establishes (ii).

(iii) is immediate from the definitions.

\square

12.2
Free products

The differentials in enriched dgl’s
(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})
and
L^{\prime\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
extend uniquely to a differential,
\partial,
in the free product
L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime\prime}
(recalled in §10.2). Then
(L=L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime\prime},\partial)
is an enriched dgl, the
free product
of
(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})
and
(L^{\prime\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
.

Proposition 12.6
.

(i)

Any two morphisms,
\iota^{\prime},\iota^{\prime\prime}:(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime}),(L^{\prime\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\to(E,\partial_{E})
extend uniquely to a morphism

(L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime\prime},\partial)\to(E,\partial_{E}).

(ii)

If
(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})
and
(\land Q,d_{0}+d_{1})
are the semi-quadratic models of
(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})
and
(L^{\prime\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
, then there is a bihomogeneous quasi-isomorphism

\textstyle{(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{0}+d_{1}).}

(iii)

Denote by
(L,\partial)
the edgl corresponding to
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
. The edgl morphisms
L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime}\to L
corresponding to the surjections
\land V\to\land W,\land Q
then extend to an isomorphism

(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})\,\widehat{\amalg}\,(L^{\prime\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(L,\partial).

proof.
(i) This follows from Lemma
10.1
since by construction the morphism
L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime\prime}\to E
is compatible with the differentials.

(ii) The proof of Proposition
10.1
provides a quasi-isomorphism

\varphi:(\land V,d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{1})

in which
(\land V,d_{1})
is a quadratic Sullivan algebra and
\varphi:V\to W\oplus Q
. Let
L
be the homotopy Lie algebra of
(\land V,d_{1})
. Then (Proposition
10.3
) the surjections
\land V\to\land W,\land Q
induce an isomorphism,

L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}L,

of enriched Lie algebras.

In particular,
\partial^{\prime}
and
\partial^{\prime\prime}
extend to a differential,
\partial
, in
L
. This in turn defines a differential
d_{0}
in
\land V
for which
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
is a semi-quadratic Sullivan algebra and
\varphi\circ d_{0}=d_{0}\circ\varphi
. Filtering by degree
-
wedge produces a morphism of spectral sequences converging from
H(\varphi,d_{1})
to
H(\varphi,d_{0}+d_{1})
. Thus

\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\to(\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})\times_{\mathbb{Q}}(\land Q,d_{0}+d_{1})

is a quasi-isomorphism.

(iii) This is immediate from the construction in (ii).

\square

13
Profree dgl’s

As in Part II,
\mathbb{L}_{T}
denotes the free graded Lie algebra freely generated by a subspace
T=T_{\geq 0}
. At the start of Part II a complete enriched structure in
T
is extended to a natural enriched Lie algebra structure in
\mathbb{L}_{T}
, and for which
T
is a closed subspace. Then, by definition, a
profree Lie algebra
is a complete enriched Lie algebra of the form
L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
. In this case
L
is the direct sum,

L=T\oplus L^{(2)},

in which
T
is closed in
L
. Finally Theorem 1, together with Lemma
6.2
, establishes the equivalence of the following three conditions on a complete enriched Lie algebra,
L
, and its quadratic model
(\land V,d_{1})
:

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}(i)&L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\mbox{ is profree}.\\
(ii)&H(\land V,d_{1})=\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\,\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}).\\
(iii)&H^{[2]}(\land V,d_{1})=0.\end{array}\right\}

(Here
H(\land V,d_{1})=\oplus_{k}H^{[k]}(\land V,d_{1})
is the decomposition induced by wedge degree.)

Definition.
A
profree dgl
is a dgl
(L,\partial)
in which
L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
is profree.

Proposition 13.1
.

(i)

An enriched dgl
(L,\partial)
admits a surjective quasi-isomorphism from a profree dgl
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)
.

(ii)

If
\rho:(E,\partial)\to(F,\partial)
is a surjective quasi-isomorphism of enriched dgl’s, then any morphism
g:(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)\to(F,\partial)
from a profree dgl lifts to provide a commutative diagram

\textstyle{(E,\partial)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{(F,\partial).}

(iii)

Any profree dgl
(L,\partial)
is the free product
(L,\partial)=(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})\,\widehat{\amalg}\,(L^{\prime\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
of a profree dgl satisfying
H(L^{\prime})=0
and a minimal profree dgl
(L^{\prime\prime},\partial^{\prime\prime})
.

proof.
(i). Let
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
be the semi-quadratic model of
(L,\partial)
. We construct by induction on
n
semi-quadratic Sullivan models of the form
(\land V\otimes\land W(n),d_{0}+d_{1})
with
W(0)\subset W(1)\subset\dot{W}(n)\subset W(n+1)\dots
, and such that

(1)

The induced map
H^{[2]}(\land V\otimes\land W(n),d_{1})\to H^{[2]}(\land V\otimes\land W(n+1),d_{1})
is zero.

(2)

The induced map
H(V\oplus W(n),d_{0})\to H(V\oplus W(n+1),d_{0})
is an isomorphism.

Here
H^{[2]}
denotes as before the component of the cohomology of wedge degree 2.
Suppose
\land V\otimes\land W(n)
has been defined. Then let
Z=s^{-1}H^{[2]}(\land V\otimes\land W(n),d_{1})
,
W(n+1)=W(n)\oplus Z\oplus d_{0}(Z)
, and for
x=s^{-1}\Omega\in Z
, we choose a
d_{1}
-cycle
\omega
with
[\omega]=\Omega
, and we define
d_{1}(x)=\omega
and
d_{1}d_{0}x=-d_{0}\omega
.

Denote
W=\cup_{n}W(n)
. Since
H^{[2]}=0
, by Lemma
6.2
,
(\land V\otimes\land W,d_{0}+d_{1})
is the semi-quadratic model of a profree dgl
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)
, and the injection
\land V\to\land V\otimes\land W
corresponds to a surjective quasi-isomorphism
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)\to(L,\partial)
.

(ii) Since
T
is closed and
\rho
is a surjective coherent map there is a coherent linear map
\alpha:T\to E
such
that
\rho\circ\alpha=id
. Moreover, since
\rho
is a coherent surjective quasi-isomorphism,
H(\mbox{ker}\,\rho)=0
. Thus there is a coherent linear map
\tau:\mbox{ker}\,\rho\cap\mbox{ker}\,\partial\to\mbox{ker}\,\rho
such that
\partial\circ\tau=id
.

Now assume
h
has been defined in
T_{<k}
, and therefore also in
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T_{\leq k}}
. Then
\sigma\circ\partial_{T}-\partial\circ\alpha:T_{k}\to\mbox{ker}\,\rho\cap\mbox{ker}\,\partial
. Extend
h
to
T_{k}
by setting

hx=\alpha x+\tau(h\partial_{T}-\partial\alpha)(x).

It is immediate that the extension to
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T_{\leq n}}
is a morphism and that
\rho\circ h=g
.

(iii). Denote by
\overline{\partial}
the quotient differential in
L/L^{(2)}
. Then (cf. Proposition
2.1
)

L/L^{(2)}=S\oplus\overline{\partial}S\oplus H,

where
S,\overline{\partial}S
and
H
are closed subspaces,
\overline{\partial}:S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\overline{\partial}S
, and
\overline{\partial}(H)=0
. Now the isomorphism
T\cong L/L^{(2)}
from a closed direct summand of
L^{(2)}
yields a decomposition

T=P\oplus\partial P\oplus Q,

in which
\partial:P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\partial P
and
\partial:Q\to L^{(2)}
. It follows that
L=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{(T\oplus\partial P\oplus Q)}
.

Moreover, filtering the Lie bracket length provides a spectral sequence converging from
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{Q}
to
H(L)
. This implies that division by
T
and
\partial T
yields a quasi-isomorphism

\rho:(L,\partial)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{Q},\partial_{Q})

where
\partial_{Q}
is the quotient differential.

Finally, by (ii)
\rho
admits a splitting
\sigma:(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{Q},\partial_{Q})\to(L,\partial)
and it is immediate from the construction that

id\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\sigma:\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{P\oplus\partial P)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{Q},\partial_{Q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(L,\partial).

Since
\partial:Q\to\mathbb{L}_{Q}^{(2)}
, this completes the proof.

\square

Given a profree dgl,
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)
, division by
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
is a coherent surjection

\rho:(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)\to(T,\overline{\partial}).

Proposition 13.2
.

Suppose
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)
is a profree dgl with semi-quadratic Sullivan model
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
, and let
\xi:\land^{\geq 1}V\to V
denote the surjection (§9).

(i)

Any coherent chain map
(T,\overline{\partial})\to(L,\partial)
into an enriched complete dgl extends uniquely to a morphism
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)\to(L,\partial)
.

(ii)

H(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1},d_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{\geq 1}(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
.

(iii)

The isomorphism (
12
),
s(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(V,d_{0})^{\vee}
, extends to the commutative diagram

\textstyle{s(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{s\rho}
\textstyle{(V,d_{0})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi^{\vee}}
\textstyle{s(T,\overline{\partial})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1},d_{0})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(\land^{\geq 1}V,d_{0}+d_{1})^{\vee}.}

(iv)

(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)
is minimal if and only if
\overline{\partial}=0
, or equivalently, if
d_{0}|_{V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}}=0
.

(v)

H(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)=0
if and only if
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
has a closed direct summand of the form
T=T(1)\oplus\partial(T(1))
where
\partial:T(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\partial T(1)
.

Corollary.
If
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)
is minimal then
sT\cong H^{\geq 1}(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})^{\vee}
.

proof of Proposition
13.2
.
(i) This is an immediate consequence of the definition of a profree dgl.

(ii) Filtration of
\land V
by degree
-
wedge degree results in a spectral sequence converging to
H(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
. Its
E_{1}
-term and
E_{2}
-term are respectively
H(\land V,d_{1})
and
(\mathbb{Q}\oplus(V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}),d_{0})
. This proves (ii).

(iii) By Lemma
4.1
, the inclusion
V\cap\mbox{ker}\,d_{1}\to V
is dual to
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}/\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
, since
(\land V,d_{1})
is the quadratic model of
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
. This gives (iii).

(iv) By definition,
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)
is minimal if and only if
\partial:\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}^{(2)}
. This gives (iv).

(v) If
H(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)=0
it follows from the isomorphism
s(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial)\cong(V,d_{0})^{\vee}
that
H(V,d_{0})=0
. This then implies that
(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
is a contractible Sullivan algebra, and therefore has zero homology in positive degrees. Now (ii) and (iii) combine to give
H(T,\overline{\partial})=0
.

Write
T=T(1)\oplus\overline{\partial}T(1)
and observe that it follows that

\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}=\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(1)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{\overline{\partial}T(1)}.

Now it follows from the Corollary to Lemma
3.1
that

\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T(1)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{\partial T(1)}\to\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}

is an isomorphism, and this is clearly an isomorphism of dgl’s.

\square

Proposition 13.3
.

Let
f:(L,\partial)\to(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})
be a morphism of profree dgl’s with corresponding bihomogeneous morphism

\varphi:(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})\longleftarrow(\land V^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{0}+d^{\prime}_{1})

of their semi-quadratic Sullivan algebras. Then the following three conditions are equivalent

(i)

H(\overline{f})
is an isomorphism

(ii)

H(f)
is an isomorphism

(iii)

H(\varphi)
is an isomorphism.

proof.
It follows from the diagram in Proposition
13.2
(iii) that
H(\overline{f})
is an isomorphism if and only if
H(\varphi)
is an isomorphism. Moreover, by Proposition
13.2
(iii),
H(f)
is an isomorphism if and only if
H(\varphi,d_{0})
is an isomorphism. Finally,
\varphi:(\land V^{\prime},d_{0}+d_{1})\to(\land V,d_{0}+d_{1})
induces a morphism of minimal Sullivan algebras of the form

\psi:(\land H(V^{\prime},d_{0}),\widehat{d})\longrightarrow(\land H(V,d_{0}),\widehat{d}).

Here the linear map induced from
\psi
by division by
\land^{\geq 2}
is
H(\varphi,d_{0})
. Therefore,

\begin{array}[]{ll}H(\varphi,d_{0})\mbox{ is an isomorphism }&\Longleftrightarrow\psi\mbox{ is an isomorphism}\\
&\Longleftrightarrow H(\psi)\mbox{ is an isomorphism }\\
&\Longleftrightarrow H(\varphi)\mbox{ is an isomorphism.}\end{array}

\square

Corollary.
If
L
and
L^{\prime}
are minimal then the conditions of Proposition
13.3
are equivalent to each of the conditions

(i)f\mbox{ is an isomorphism}\hskip 14.22636pt(ii)\varphi\mbox{ is an isomorphism.}

proof.
If
L
and
L^{\prime}
are minimal then
\overline{f}=H(\overline{f})
is an isomorphism. Thus
f
maps a closed direct summand,
T
, of
L^{(2)}
isomorphically onto a direct summand,
T^{\prime}
, of
(L^{\prime})^{(2)}
. Since
f
is coherent,
T^{\prime}
is closed and
L^{\prime}
is the closure of
\mathbb{L}_{T^{\prime}}
. It follows that
f
is an isomorphism. Since, in particular,
\varphi:(\land V^{\prime},d_{1})\to(\land V,d_{1})
is the morphism of quadratic Sullivan algebras corresponding to
f
it follows that
\varphi
is also an isomorphism.
\square

14
Profree extensions and homotopy

14.1
Profree extensions

Definition.

1.

A
profree extension
is an inclusion of the form
i:(L,\partial)\to(L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S},\partial)
of enriched dgl’s.

2.

The
quotient
of a profree extension is the profree dgl
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S},\overline{\partial})
obtained by dividing by the ideal generated by
L
. The profree extension is
minimal
if the quotient is a minimal profree dgl.

3.

The
secondary gradation
in
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}
is that uniquely determined by assigning to an iterated bracket of Lie elements
x_{i}\in L
and
y_{i}\in S
the secondary degree,
\sum\mbox{deg}\,y_{i}
.

4.

The
spectral sequence
of the profree extension is the spectral sequence obtained by the decreasing filtration by secondary degree.

5.

Two profree extensions
L\to L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}
,
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}
define a single profree extension
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S\oplus T}
and

L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S\oplus T}=L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}=L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}.

Profree extensions have the five important properties listed in Proposition
14.1
below. The first three follow immediately from the definitions. The fourth follows from the same argument as the proof of Proposition
13.1
(ii). The fifth follows from the definition (§10.2) of free products.

Proposition 14.1
.

Let
(L,\partial)\to(L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S},\partial)
be a profree extension.

(i)

The spectral sequence of the extension converges from
H(L)\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}
to
H(L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S})
.

(ii)

A morphism
f:(L,\partial)\to(L^{\prime},\partial^{\prime})
of enriched dgl’s determines a unique differential in
L^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}
for which
f\,\widehat{\amalg}\,id_{\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}}
is a morphism of profree extensions.

(iii)

If
f
is a quasi-isomorphism so is
f\,\widehat{\amalg}\,id_{\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}}
.

(iv)

In a commutative diagram of enriched dgl morphisms,

\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{F,}

suppose that
\iota
is the inclusion of a profree extension and that
\rho
is a surjective quasi-isomorphism. Then there is a morphism

h:L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}\longrightarrow E

such that
h\circ\iota=f
and
g\circ h=g
.

(v)

The natural map
L\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}\to\varprojlim_{n}L/L^{(n)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 14.2
.

Suppose
\varphi:(E,\partial_{E})\to(F,\partial_{F})
is a morphism of enriched dgl’s. Then

(i)

\varphi
factors as

\textstyle{\varphi:(E,\partial_{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{(E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S},\partial)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{(F,\partial_{F})}

in which
\iota
is the inclusion of a profree extension and
\rho
is a surjective quasi-isomorphism.

(ii)

\varphi
also factors as

\textstyle{\varphi:(E,\partial_{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{(E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{(F,\partial_{F})}

in which
\lambda
is the inclusion of a minimal profree extension and
\gamma
is a quasi-isomorphism.

proof.
(i) Proposition
13.1
gives a surjective quasi-isomorphism
\sigma:(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S(0)},\partial)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(F,\partial)
. By Proposition
12.6
(i) this extends to the morphism

\textstyle{\rho(0)=\varphi\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\sigma:(E,\partial_{E})\,\widehat{\amalg}\,(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S(0)},\partial)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(F,\partial_{F}).}

By construction, both
\varphi\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\sigma
and
H(\varphi\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\sigma)
are surjective.

Next we construct
S(1)=\oplus_{n\geq 1}S(1)_{n}
and a sequence of profree extensions

E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S(0)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L(n)=\left[E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S(0)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L(n-1)\right]\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S(1)_{n}}.

These will be equipped with morphisms

\rho(n):E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S(0)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,L(n)\longrightarrow F

with
\rho(n)
extending
\rho(n-1)
and
H_{<n}(\rho(n))
an isomorphism. Then
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S(1)}=\varinjlim_{n}L(n)
and the
\rho(n)
define a quasi-isomorphism

\textstyle{\rho:E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{F}

with
S=S(0)\oplus S(1)
.

The construction is by induction on
n
. If
\rho(n-1)
is constructed we may, since
\rho(n-1)
is coherent, write

\mbox{ker}\,\rho(n-1)\cap\mbox{ker}\,\partial=C\oplus\partial(\mbox{ker}\,\rho(n-1))

in which
C=C_{\geq n-1}
is a closed subspace. Set
S(1)_{n}=sC_{n-1}
and define
\partial|_{S(1)_{n}}
to be the isomorphism
S(1)_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{n-1}
. Then extend
\rho(n-1)
to
\rho(n)
by setting
\rho(n)(S(1)_{n})=0
.

(ii) Denote by
\rho:(E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S},\partial)\to F
the construction of (i), and denote by
\partial_{S}
the quotient differential in
\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}
. By Proposition
13.1
(iii) we may write

(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S},\partial_{S})=(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial_{T})\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{(U\oplus\partial_{S}U)}

with
\partial_{S}:U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\partial_{S}U
and
(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T},\partial_{T})
a minimal profree dgl. Filtering by the closed ideals
E^{(n)}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}
shows (Proposition
14.1
(v)) that

E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,(\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{(U\oplus\partial U)})\longrightarrow E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}

is an isomorphism of dgl’s.

This provides the commutative diagram of dgl morphisms

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{U\oplus\partial U})\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}}

in which the spectral sequence shows that
\psi
is a quasi-isomorphism. Thus
h
exists by Proposition
14.1
(iv). In this case
h
must also be a quasi-isomorphism, and the composite

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{F}

is the factorization of
\varphi
.

\square

Next suppose that

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{E}}
\textstyle{E\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{F}}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}}

is a commutative diagram of morphisms of enriched dgl’s. Then extend the lower row to

\textstyle{E^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{P\oplus\partial P}\,\widehat{\amalg}\,\overline{\mathbb{L}}_{S^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}}

by setting
P=F^{\prime}_{\geq 1}
,
